Clive, when a, boy, was once walking with a schoolfellow through Drayton Market, and the two lads stopped to look at a butcher killing a calf: . "'Dear me, Bobby," says the lad, "I would not be a butcher for all the world." "Why, I should not much like it," said Clive—"it's a dirty, beggarly business; but I'd a plaguy deal rather be a butcher than a calf. TILE FORCE OF EXAMPLE.—Mrs. Nagger (to servant) : "I hear you're 'going to be married. Jane, so I suppose you will want to leave?" Servant: "No, mum, I've broken it off. A_ seeing the life that you and master lead, I've decided never to marry, mum l" THE LANGUAGE OF LOVE.—It is ill-natured of the neighbours to say that Tomkins has changed in his feelings towards his wife. If there is any change, it is only consistent with the fight of time. When they were first married, he called her, "You little puss !" That would be ridiculous now, and therefore it is, no doubt, that he now calls her. "You old cat." GOOD QUARTERS. Two Irish soldiers being quartered in a borough in the West of England-, got into conversation respecting the quarters. "Haw," said. Macarthy, "are you quartered?" "Pretty well." "What part of the house do you sleep in? Upstairs in the garret, perhaps?' "The garret, LS it? No, an O'Brien would never sleep in the garret." "Where, then ?"- "Sure I don't know what they call the place at all, but if Ow house were upside ,down, I should be in the cellar."' - a- Cumin-ix AND COOKERY.—" Oh, yes," said the eldest Miss Culture, the other evening, "I break- fasted with Mrs. Brainwaite, and we enjoyed a ~.; delicious- repast—excellent coffee, splendid. bread, and piscatorial gt-obes." A useful bag for holding soiled handkerchiefs, _Collars, _ cuffs,,&?,„ is made of holland, worked on one side with a disign-M-Oblitie linen thread, or crewel, and on'the other With the initials of the owner. The two sides are 18 in. long, 14 in. wide at the bottom, gradttally eloping‘to.9 in. at the top. A straight piece, 6 in. wide joins the two sides, and the joining is covered by a cotton cord. Brass rings are sewn at distances 1 in. apart, and . 21 in. from the top, on the wrong side. A cord is , drawn through the rings by which to draw up the top of the bag. GENERAL NEWS.

Messrs. Nelson and Co., tea dealers—a firm consisting oi three•partners—Were remanded at Manchester on Monday on a charge .f holding a lottery. The pro- secutors are local Grocer's Association, and it was stated that the modus operandi was to give a coupon to every purchaser ot a pound of tea, which entitled him to take part in a ballot for £5, with the chance of winning another £5. At Tainwoith Police Court, on Monday, Charles Henry Dent, plumber, and Frank Cannock, mechanic, the senders of the -bogus bombs which created such a scare, were fined £4B each, or three months' imprisonment in default. ' 'rile Manchester City Council has adopted a somawhat novel scheme for the housing of the working classes. It is proposed to clear about five acres of an over-crowded and unhealthy area in the centre of the city,. and erec• thereon blocks of.workmen's dwellings, making liberal provision for playgrounds, &c. 7 • A. NE Ngt game was mentioned .t The ,lately of a very simple description, and Father, I should say, a variation upon the welliknown " drawing a blind pig." This arnentthent consists in cutting out a donkey in paper minus the tail, which is cut out separately. The donky is then stuck on to a window curtain or the portiere, and the players are blindfolded each in-turn and try to pin the animal's tail on'in -itii 'Win:rat position. S The results are often.very amusing., . GAME OF " STIPK.".—A ganie called " stock " or stick is played as follows : row of chairs, put ' close togedier, but facing alternately in opposite directions, is placed in the centre:: of the room; the players, numbering one more than the chairs, fill them completely, leaving one person standing. • This one takes a stick, and, ms.ecjiing around the raps with it on- the floor in front of one chair, then another, thus summoning the occu- pants to follow him, until the entire company are marching in Indian file behind the leader; sud- denly he calls out, "Stock !" and sits down quickly ; everybody tries to -secure a seat, but necessarily one is left; this one then takes up the "stock," and in turn crowds out somebody else • and so it goes on: The game is healthful, affording moderate exercise; social, since the company keeps changing places, and laughing, and chatting, whether seated or walking; and funny, when the sudden scrambling for seats begins. • A "BLIND QUADRILLE."—Four couples having

been blindfolded, they attempt to -go throne' prettily and correctly—but in vain—all the antics they are supposed to have learnt from early child- hood. Alas I they fail miserably, and wobble about . in out-of-the-way -corners of. the room in a condition at once helpless and ridiculous. For example, in the "Ladies' chain" business they imagine in the innocence of their hearts that they are performing gracefully. Oh, ‘'dear no! They are scattered about the room in Vroken fragments, vainly seeking applause and admiration. The same grotesque amtics'are displayed in the genteel and praiseworthy endeavour to "right" them- selves in the stately ‘" Pas Seul" in -the second figur . Their attempts to look proper and digni- fied are futile, 'and the performance is often con- cluded in an obscure corner of the drawing-room! But it is let for the "Finale" to exhibit muddle and hopeless confusion. It is needless to add that the finish is entirely deprived of that method and harmony which at the outset was so promising. AN ARITHMETICAL GAME.—Quick- witted players will be interested in an arithmetical game called "buzz." All ihose taking part are seated. in % circle or uround the room. One person begins by saying, "One," the next, " Two " and then the counting continues around the circle, but when vie number seven is reached, or any multiple of seven (as 'l4, 21, &c.), it must not be given, but in its place the person says, "Buzz," and the following number is counted by the next player. On the failure of anyone to say "Buzz' at the proper time, or shoull he say when- not called for, he is dropped from the circle, and thus the game pro- ceeds, usually beginning at " one " each time a person fails, until only one counter remains, and he is the winner. The game may be made more , coMplicated for older chi:dren by substituting the word "buzz" for any number having the word "seven" in it (such as 17, 27, &c.), or even for those double numbers (such as 16, 25, 43, &c.), which when added together make seven. THE FEATHER GAME. --The players are seatedin circle, with theil chairs cloSe together. The leader takes a piece of goose or swan's-down, and blows- it upwards towards the centre of the circle. The company are now expected td keep it afloat with their breath, its falling to the ground involving a forfeit from the person on whom, or nearest to , whom.it falls. A similar game can be played on a polished round table with a loose ball of wool.

CAPITAL" CHAT.

There seems no doubt that Edgar Musgrave, the blind pianist now perambulating the West-end of London, is . what he represents himself to be— namely, a gentleman of independent means who, through no misconduct of his own, has come down into the world. He is the son of the Rev. George Musgrave, lord of the manor of ShillingtOn; the grandson of Mr. George Musrave, Who was high sheriff of .Bedfordshire in 18:36, and nephew of Mr. Henry Musgrave, J.P., of High Wycombe. In 1862 he succeeded his father at the Manor House, Mil- lington, where he resided until 1370. F r several years he officiated as organist at the. village church, and his wife stills lives in the locality. His mother, - who was the daughter of Mr. Alfred Oakes, amem- ber of the Indian Government, is interred in Mil- lington churchyard. Tnexe, facts are vouched for by Mr. John lienapston, of North Ley House, Mil- lington, who succeeded Edgar Musgrave as tenant the Manor House.

SO t i. 17-• There is a rumour in quarters usually well in- v.:. formed, that it is the intention of Lord Rosehery to 'create no more Peers. It is stated, and, underthe- circumstances, the statement must he repeated here with some reserve (though the probabilities are all :on the side of its being literally true), that the Premier came to this determination as soon as the .4,.;Cabinet had decided to enter upon the anti-Lords crusacle, holding the strong conviction that it would be inconsistent with the policy thus solemly and .deliberately undertaken, to give any further countenance to the hereditary principle in the Legi.:lature. There are now about forty (not thirty) Liberal Peers.

The enormous power of the Rothschilds is such as Kings might envy. In these commercial days it is the bankers who make war possible, or preserve peace The Paris house of Rothschild withdrew the Russian loan some time ago on the eve of its being launched because of the Jewish persecutions. 'They are now willing to raise money for the Czar, but it appears that they have been able to dictate their terms. It is confidently stated in Jewish circles that assurances have now been given which bind the Russian Government not further to harass the Jews within its dominions. There will not be any striking demonstration of its clemency, but the mot cl'ordre will be given that the Czar will not view with any satisfaction the continuance of the oppressive restrictions and expulsions of the Jews. The • Rothschild s have, in fact, extended effectual protection to the largest section of the Jewish race.

There was not a whisper in literary circles here that Robert Louis Stevenson was in worse health '.than usual, and the unlooked-for announcement of 'his death will send a shock through the whole Eng- lish literary world. Wit,i him passes away ';_,ne of „ :the purest and greatest of our writers, and his death will be felt as a personal loss throughout a very wide body of literary workers among whom he had many warm friends. Everyone also to whom pure and elevating literature is a matter of interest in these days of the New Woman, with her unwhole- some ethics and ideals, will acknowledge that Illnglish literature has suffered a grievous loss. Mr. Stevenson had a very slender hold of life, but, like s•-. -.James Adlington Symons and Richard Jefferies, he toiled on in noble faith and persistence till the slelicate thread was at last severed. But his bodily suffering never dimmed his genius nor abridged his power, and the world will read with interest the novel on which be was engaged, and which was far •sk advanced, when the at al blow came. It is not long since he gave us a new and delightful romance, and It is not a month since the issue of a complete series -of his works began. Now, before the second ,a;i:soltime has seen the light, he has been cut down in the flower of his life, and one of the most perfect of i our prose writers has been laid to his long rest on the summit of a lonely peak in the South Pacific. Mr. Stevenson's was one of what I may call the redeeming intellects of the time; it, was one of the offsets that the age can offer to the flood of Neuro- ticism and diseased ethics and art which has set in upon it from so many quarters,

• - Private letters, giving accounts of Lord Randolph Churchill's tour and of his health, now hint in un- mistakable terms that the affliction from which he is suffering is developing into something serious. They are silent, however, as to the nature or extent of the attack, or, if they speak of either, the fact is not yet made public. Probably nb one who has listened to Lord Randolph either in the House of Commons oi on a public platform for the last two years will be ready to question the suggestion the . - letters convey, as his tongue at time seemed to be quite unable to perform its function. He has not, it is said,beiaefited by his long tour, but the attack, whatever form it is assuming, is not of such a hatufe as to prevent him from pursuing his travels, or to induce him to return home. lie resists the pressure which his friends put upon him to come back to England, and is determined to complete hi travels in the East.

The first act of union among the Methodists of Australasia has just been consummated. The 'churches belonging to the Bible Uhri*tian demonina- ,*:: tion in Queensland, having obtained permission from ths British Conference, have united with the Wesleyan body in anticipation of the general amalgamation of the Methodist bodies. Two of the ministers, les.:.rs. Bennett and Hooper, have, how- ever, decided not to enter the Wesleyan ministry, but to leave Queensland for other colonies. The formation of a United Methodist Church for Queen- sland will have its effect on Methoiism throughout Australasia and in time on the churches in Great Britain.

-- Trades unionism, at least of the older school, and the Independent Labour party are on the eve of a struggle which will probably split up the working men into two sections, each to pursue fora time its own separate path. The revision of the constitu- tion of the Congress which the Parliamentary Com- mittee has just made is intended to have the effect of excluding the of the Independent Labour party, and though this will strike at some old orthodox trades unionists like Mr. Henry Broadhurst, that is an unavoidable consequence and not an object. It aims directly at the exclusion of Mr. Keir Hardie, Mr. Tom Munn. and others who come under the category of neither being engaged at present in their trade nor being paid officials of their union. Paid secretaries like Mr. Woods and Mr. Fenwick _ would remain members of the Congress' and so probably would Mr. John Burns, but Mr. Burns's case is doubtful. Nor does the revision stop here, for by the new rules only one delegate may be sent, who may give a bunch of votes for the whole union, though nearly half the union may be in opposition, - instead of giving as formerly one vote for every , thousand members, provided delegates proportion- J_ ate to the members represented were in attendance.. Putting these two changes together, their effect is to place the Congress completely under the con- trol of a clique of prdminent trades unionists, whereby probably the opinions of one-half of the working men throughout the kingdom will be un- represented, or misrepresented, and to establish one of the narrowest oligarchies on the ruins of the present democratic system of voting.

-- For the third time the Royal Albert Hall has been placed at the disposal of the editor of "Truth "for the annual toy and doll show. The present show is the fifteenth of the series. The grand total of from 26,000 to 27,000 toys has been reached. The principal feature of the exhibition is once more a very extensive and comprehensive display of dressed dolls of all sorts and sizes. Some 4,000 dolls have been prepared by the lady readers of "Truth," 4-, , , including SO large ones dressed in the most elabor- ate manner. Whilst the display of dressed dolls frog surpasses in extent and interest any previous exlei-; -14s=-• bition, it does not eclipse the home-made toys, al- ways an interesting feature. This year the toys show advance in point of ingenuity and skill. Foremost among these are a wonderfully made house-boat, complete with every appurtenance, a golfing scene, a circus, and "a garden party," the latter made by a girl of 15.. Topics of present in- terest are well represented, such as election scenes, " the new woman," and so forth. "Constantinople . at the Albert Hall" deserves special • mention, the - .scenes including over 70 dolls. `IHE EXAMINER, SATURDAY DECEMBER 22 1894.

ELECTION OF GUARDIANS AND DISTRICT corxciLLons.

They required great reforms in their workhouse infir- maries, where teere was s much helpless suffering, and it was here that s !1)11,2.nce would be for good. But the saddesto ' if a Teeierk no was -he workhouse proper. "as self-rife- t wasted humanity—e-ee ed eea exac le Ik-' efenselves, with the same teepAcity Sr enjoyment, the same pswer of suffering, the same affections, but living in a world always under a shadow. There was no place in the whale world where a tender sympathy and tact was more required than in the great dreary monetonooe world which existed within the walls of a workhouse. Therefore, it seemed to her that the work of a woman was all important and absolutely necessary. The influ- ence of women upon public life conduced very much to increased morality and purity. Women had less tern pta- Urns than men to make public matters serve their own _,ivate interests.- (Hear, hear.) -She did not think they had as strong a commercial instinct as men, but Mrs. Fawcett, speaking in Liverpool some few months ago, said that it seemed to her a great pity that the economy, urity and self-denial of womeli should not be more generally utilised in the world. Much good work had been done by men guardians, but she thought to, make the work thoroughly harmenious they needed the co- operation of women. A great deal was said that the work would take women from their homes, but the really busy women always found time for everything. She hoped that all those present would do their utmost to return their lady candidate to the head of the poll. (Loud applause.) She also hoped that they would re- turn every other candidate who was straightforward,and prepared to make the poor-law system—instead of be- coming; like a tyrannous task master—more just, wise, and beneficent. (Applause.) Mr. JAMES EVANS said that he rose to propose a resolu- tion, but before he read it, he wished to make a few observations. For a considerable time, the admistration of poor law had engaged his attention. The present method of admimistration bordered upon—he would not like to say cruelty—but upon everything which tended to a pauperising influence. He thought that it was a condition which should not be. He thought it was hard indeed for persons who had struggled through the battle of life for 40 or 50 years, to be compelled by the sheer force of circumstances to end their days in the work- house. He thought that the pauperising influences ought to be removed. He eontended that when he had paid his rates while he was in his health. and strength, if he were compelled to go to the workhouse he ought to be treated well. There were many reasons why they ought to have lady guardians. In the sick wards there were many women and children, and it was more proper for a woman to go there than a man. The resolution he had to propose was the following "It is highly desirable that the claims of women should be represented upon the Board of Guardians, and this meeting is of opinion that Mrs. Clay is a very suitable candidate for that position, and pledges itself to use every possible lawf effort to secure her election on Monday next." (Ap- plause). Councillor PLUMPTON seconded the resolution. He said that they would all admit from what they had heard that night that there was a necessity for a lady guardian in the Union. It had been shown to them, that there were duties to perform which could not be as well per- 'formed by a man. There were many duties which were not performed, simply because of there being no lady guardian in the Union. There were many things which required altering in the Workhouse. When a man and his wife were compelled to seek shelter in that place, they ought not to be separated as was the case at pre- sent. They had a candidate before them who would give satisfaction in every way. Mrs. Clay had not sought that position herself. She had not any desire or ambition out of selfishness. He and other friends had talked the matter over for some months, and they con- sidered it was necessary to have a lady guardian, and in going through the various names in the town, they came to the conclusion that for the Whitecross ward, Mrs. Clay was the right candidate. It was not for any political motive that they brought her outebut simply because they believed there was the work to bedone by a woman. When he took the nomination papers for Mrs. Clay to Mr. Sutton, Mr.. Sutton said that he was glad that a lady was coming forward, and that it was very necessary that they should have a lady guardian, as they were doing grand work in other towns. He there- fore thought that Warrington ought to have a lady guardian. Mrs. Clay did not claim to be a great speaker but she was a worker. kApplause. ) Many knew how hard she worked in the soup kitchen during the distress last year. Those were the kind of persons they wanted for lady guardians. Some said that a woman's place was at home, but if all the women had stayed at home how much good work which had been accomplished, would have been left undone? They would remember the late Mayoress.—Mrs. Greenall, whenever there was any work to be done, was there to do it, and every- body respected her for it, irrespective of politics. The candidate on that platform had joined her at the work,. He, asked the Whitecross ward, whichlwas always fore- most in proeress, to set a good example to the town by returning Mrs. Clay at the head of the poll by a sub- stantial majority on Monday, and thus encourage other wards to bring forward lady candidates. (Applause.) Mrs. BRIGHT said that she was surprised to hear that aged couples were not allowed to remain together in the workhouse. In the West Derby Union there was a num- ber of tenernentsbuilt for the purpose of sheltering those people. Mr.A. E. BENJAMIN supported the resolution. He was glad that the Whitecross Ward was the pioneer in this movement. Lady candidates ought to have been elected years ago, but if they had come forward previously they- would have stood no chance of being elected. Among other reforms he was in favour of, was that the pauper uniforms ought not to be tolerated, and that no man or woman ought to be compelled to wear this badge ef de- gradation. The cost of administration ought to be re- duced. For every shilling of the ratepayers' money which was spent,it cost is. id. to spend it. The separa- tion of aged couples ought not to be tolerated, and he thought that an expression ought to go from that meet- ing demanding that this should not be allowed in the future. It was against all biblical and Christian teach- ing. He trusted that they as pioneers in the lady can- didate movement would not relax their efforts, but that in time they would return a majority of lady candidates to the Beard of Guardians. Mr.R. ISHERWOOD here arose, and said that it was not true that aged couples were separated if they did not wish it. It was a wring impression that people got. They lived together nine years ago when he was a Guar- dian, and they lived together to-day. Mr. I. HAYWARD supported the resolution proposed by Mr. Evans. He said that he had no fear of the result. He thought that they as workingmen ought to support Mrs. Clay, not only because of the principle involved, but for her English pluck in being the pioneer in that movement. He thought it was a credit and honour to the Whitecross ward that it should have the first lady candidate. He had no doubt that they would be able to give as eend an account of themselves as it was possible for any ward to do. (Applause.) Mrs. PIERPOINT was about to put the resolution of confidence in Mrs. Clay to the meeting, when. Mr. John Oaxnex (a member of the Trades Council) said that lie would like to ask Mrs. Clay whether, if she was returned to the Guardians, she would support a resolution that contracts of the Board af Guardians should not be given to any employer expect those wtio employed trade unionists. Mrs. CLAY: Certainly.

Mr. OAKDEN then asked whether she was in favour of using the power which the Guardians had of borrowing money to provide work for the unemployed in the town . .

an district.

A gentleman in the audience said that he did not think the Guardians had that power. but Mrs. Clay said that she would give it a favourable consideration, and Mr. Oakden expressing himself satisfied with the answers given, the resolution Was passed unanimously. Councillor SHAW aILERN was delighted to see from thk meeting that Mrs. Clay had such a good chance of win- ning the election. He was very much surprised to see her opponent at the meeting, and he was very much surprised that he should have opposed her at all. She could give attention to the details of the Board as well as the men, and there were plenty of men on the Board of Guardians to look after the financial department. The children at Padgate required the oversight of a lady guardian. They wanted to make them respectable citizens, and everyone knew the influence of a lady in those mattes. He hoped that Mr Isherwood would see his way to withdraw before he was completely crushed on Monday next. He proposed that a hearty vote of thanks be accorded Mrs. Bright for her speech that night.

Mr. G. SAVAGE seconded the motion which was car- ried unanimously. Mrs. G. ALLTON proposed a vote of thanks to Mrs. Pierpoint for presiding that night. She said that she hoped they would find willing hands to put Mrs. Clay in, to look after the poor women and children in the wards. If they saw what she saw every week, they would only be too thankful to put Mrs. Clay at the head of the poll to look after the interests of the poor. Mrs. J. YONOE seconded the resolution. She said that she only wished some other ward had been wise enough to choose another lady candidate. There had been a good deal said that night about women working outside their homes, and she would like to say something about it, Last year in the time of distress she had staying at her home, a minister, who asked a great many questions ' about the soup kitchen. etc. She told him a great deal, , and he said that he could not see how she could do it. He thought, she must be neglecting her home very much. She asked him whether he saw anything wrong in it. (Applause.) She also told him that if her husband corn- nlained of anything, she would drop something else so as not to neglect her home. It was not the woman who tried to do something who neglected her home. It was the woman who spent her time in reading novels—(ap- plause)—those who spent evenings in the theatre or at tea drinking in their neighbours' houses. Those were the women who neglected their homes. (Applause.) Those who looked after the comforts of their fellow creatures, were almost exclusively the best mothers, and wives. It was because they had a lanze heart, good commonsense, and plenty of brains, that they were dis- posed to do the work, and to help those who were cast down. (Applause.) MEETING IN SUPPORT OF MRS. CLAY.

, A crowded and enthusiastic meeting of the electors of the Whitecross Ward vl7 t h-ld in the St. Barnabas'school- room on Thursday evening,last week in support of Mrs. Clay's candidature. The chair was occupied by Mrs. Wm. Pierpoint, and she was supported by Mrs. Alan H. Bright (Liverpool), Mrs. Clay, Mrs. H. Roberts, Mrs. J. Walker, Mrs. 1. Hayward, Mrs. G. Allton, Mrs. J. Yonge, Mrs. Joseph Owen,and Mrs. J. Shaw Green. Mr. Robert Isherwood,the opposing candidate, was present in the audience. Mrs. PIERPOINT who was received with loud applause, said that she regretted very much that Miss Bessie Dut- ton was unable to be present with them that evening. She had unfortunately another meeting to attend to. Mrs. Armitage was also not able to be present, but she had sent a letter to Mrs. Clay in which she said that she was very glad that Mrs. Clay had come forward, and she hoped they would have a good meeting. They were there that night with the object of returning Mrs. Clay, as poor law guardian. She believed that itwas gener- ally admitted that there were matters in which women were qualified to act in poor law administration very much better than men—(applause)—such as the care of the aged, of children, and ef many other domestic mat- ters. Mrs. Clay was admirably constituted to fulfil the position she sought, and she was thoroughly worthy of the confidence of the electors. (Applause.) It was gener- rally a rule that a chairman must not speak long,and she supposed that that also referred to a chairwoman, and she would at once call upon Mrs. Clay to address the meeting. Mrs. CLAY, who was received with loud cheers, said that she was sorry that they were being put to the trouble and expense .of a contested election. For some time past the necessity for women Guardians had been so generally admitted that she felt sure if her opponent (of whom she desired to speak with all respect), had carefully considered the subject, he would have _saved all turmoil by gallantly retiring from the contest. As many were aware she had been a ratepayer and resident in Whitecross ward for 20 years and it was only when the ward boundaries were altered that she found herself just outside. She therefcre claimed to know quite as much of the affairs of the ward and its particular requirements as most people. She was now seeking their, help, not for herself, Mit for the very poor —those whom trouble, sickness, old age and mis- fortune had compelled to seek shelter, either temporary or permanent in the Union Workhouse. She wanted to be of service to the poor mothers and little children in the workhouse. She could not enter into details there, but all knew that there was a large number of cases of a very painful nature, where a woman and a woman only could give that counsel and help which was so necessary and without which the trials of poverty were almost beyond human endurance As thetmother of afamilyshe desired to assist inthe train- ing of the little children in the workhouse who in many sad cases were both fatherless and motherless, and to help to remove as far as it was possible to do so, thel taint of pauperism, which so often prevented a work- house child from having a fair chance in the battle of life. She desired to obtain full and fair consideration to the unfortunate widows who sometimes sought tern- Doran, outdoor relief, and who if it was granted,eould keep their children and homes together without being forced to go into the workhouse. And why should their very old people L-these who might have lived together fo 20 or 30 years be thrust' apart the moment they enteredr the workhouse doors. This was contrary both to Divine Law and humane feeling and could be remedied without any extra cost to the 17nion. She might be asked— what can one woman do? Her reply to that was" one capable woman can do a great deal." She could at least visit the maternity and sick wards and see for her- self whether the helpless ones were being treated with proper consideration She could vote quite as intelli- gently and perhaps speak as well as some male Guar- dians. A woman's presence on the Relief Committee would at and rate have a controlling effect upon the character of the remarks made when poor women were the applicants. Finally, as women were frequently the chancellors of the exchequer in household affairs, it was not unlikely they might be able to prevent reckless expenditure 1100 TI official's salaries, as in other directions. She disclaimed all political feeling and asked for the support of all voters on behalf of those she had re- ferred to. If they elected her no effort should he want- ing on her part to justify the choice they had made. (Loud applause.) Mrs. PIERPOINT then called upon Mrs. Allan H. Bright who, she said, did not belong to any political association. Mrs. BRIGHT said she gathered from the remarks made that night that she need not apologise for not giving her allegiance to any political faith. She .was very glad to find that Mrs. Clay was standing as a non-political can- didate. Some days 920 a speaker in Liverpool, speaking on behalf of the women's suffrage m.yement, said that the movement bad three qualifications. it was reason- able, righteous, and hound to succeed. He also added that unfortunately it was a Proved case, and there was nothing against it, and therefore he could only make a doll speech. (Laughter.) She could say that night, about the return of a lady poor-law guardian, that it was reasonable, righteous, and bound to succeed. (Ap- plause.) It had succeeded in many parts of England and Wales, and also in Liverpool from whence she came. It was an extraordinary thing that the election of women as noor-law guardians should have been delayed so long. There had been great reforms effected in Liverpool, but those reforms had not been effected with- out great difficulty, and the.. were largely due to ladies.

• It has surprised many people to read in Sir Richard Owen's Life," recently published by Mr Murray, a statement that during Sir Richard's visit to Hawarden,thirty years ago, " Mr. Gladstone sang two songs." It has been thought incongruous to couple the name of the great stateAnan with so frivolous an occupation as song-singing. Never- theless it is so. Mt% taladstone,is, or used to be, a great, singer, and the possessor of a beautiful sing- ing voice. His organ is a robust one. If it had been cultivated, anrl he had devoted himself to music, he might have been one of the finest tenors of his day. Mr. Gladstone's favourite tong is "My Pretty Jane." But there be those who, in his younger days (perhaps when he was a promising youth c) fifty) have heard him sing with a deep personal sense of enjoyment nigger cu. Christy min- strel songs, with Mrs. Gladstone joining in, and he , sang them with zest and the whole-hearted earnest- ness that have distinguished every action, grand , or trivial, of his wonderful life. CARDS, MAr-LAZINES, ETC.

There can be no doubt of the fact that Messrs. Raphael, Tuck, and Sons, have done their shara to- wards educating the taste of the British public— and the American too—in works of art as repre- sented by Christmas cards,booklets, calendars, &c.; tor works of art they undoubtedly are ; designed and embellished by artists of repute, and replete with original compositions in prose and verse by men (and women) of letters. Now-a-days we are not satisfied with Christmas fare for the body, we want food for the mind as well ; sessonable,and the best of its kind, and who shall say, that this year at any rate, Messrs. Tuck and Sons have not spoiled us with choice? To particularin the nume- rous designs would be a work of time, bqt we can- not refrain from mentioning, especially, a series of designs in black and white, for which the arts of etching, engraving, photogravure, Stc., have been called upon to contribute, with the most pleasing results. Though the "black and white" cards are chaste to a degree,the designs in colours are equally so,and most artistic in execution and combination. Purchasers cannot do better than make a selection from these charming novelties, and we venture to say that in doing so they will find something to please themselves, and their friends also ; the giver as well as the receiver. T. H. ROBERTS. - Notwithstaiyling that "in Town" has, so far, been an almost, perfect produc- tion, the number for January, '9si certainly comes out ahead in the excellence of its literary and pic- torial contents. , Among the more prominent fea- tures are four different artist& ideas of Christmas Pay abroad, viz., in Canada, Australia, Paris, and Prague, cleverly showing the difference in the pastimes and employments of the inhabitants of these wide apart places,on that day; eight presenta- tion plates printed in delicate colours are presented gratis, of which the most interesting is perhaps that pourtraying George Lohmann, the popular Surrey cricketer. This ip taken from a special photograph of Lohmann in Cape Town. All the complete stories and minor illustrations are of a first-class nature, and two of the former are by John Strange Winter and S. L. de Blowitz, jun., respectively.

A MUSICAL AND LITERARY EYEING

AT RUNCORN.

-- Mr. Powell Thomas, a well-know lecturer, musician, and elocutionist, provided a concert and entertainment under the auspices of the Bethesda Literary and Social Class,on Tuesday evening. The entertainment was held in the Lecture Hall; but the inclement weather mili- tated against the success of the effort from a financial point of view, the room only being about half filled. Mr. A. R. Norman presided, and stated that a great deal was done by Mutual Improvement Societies, of wbich,he supposed, the literary society connected wirh Bethesda, was one. If they interrogated some of their greatest men they would find that many had their beginning at those Mutual Improvement Societies. They had been led to get on their feet, and interchange their ideas, with the result that they had gained confidence in them- selves and risen to the positions they now occupied. A good many things could be said in favour of those liter- ary societies. They were calculated to take the conceit out of them. They never found out their own ignorance so much as when they came to talk with other people, and discuss matters with them. It was well that it should be brought home to them,and it could not be done better than at the societies he had mentioned —Prior to commencing his entertainment, Mr. Powell Thomas said it afforded him considerable pleasure to make an appear- ance in Runcorn after an absence of something like 17 years. His object was to lend instruction with amuse- ment, and to give a love for poetry and music. The programme was of a miscellaneous character. Com- mencinc, with the performance of a selection on the piano fi"utina, which received well-merited applause, he further delighted his audience by his next contribution —the singing of "Fading Away." This was followed by an English reading, entitled " The Charity of Heart," by Eliza Cook. A clever performance on the concertina was next given. Mr. Thomas afterw trds,in graphic manner, gave a brief sketch of the career of " Edgar Allan Poe," concluding with the recital of that author's poem entitled "The Bells." In this the enter- tainer excelled in his imitation of the intonation of the various bells mentioned in the poem. The second part of the programme commenced with several selections played most efficiently on the pi ino flutina. An histori- cal recital of " Mary Queen of Scots," by Glassford and also a musical recital of " Trouble your heads with your own affairs" (Cook), were given with such thoughtful and dignified elocution as to keep the interest of the audience from the commencement tn the finish. Some popular airs including "All hail to thee Cambria," and the "March of the men of Harlech," were performed on the English concertina with musicianly taste and skill. The concluding piece was a lecturette on " Char- acter, habits and manners," which proved most interest- ing. Altogether a most enjoyable evening's entertain- ment was provided. A vote of thanks was heartily accorded the entertainer and lecturer on the proposition of the Rev. J. S. Simpson. seconded by Mr. T. Handley J.P.

The death is announced from St. Leonard's of Sir Joseph C. Lee, ceputy chairman of the Manchester Ship Canal Company. Sir Edmund Lechmere, Barr., M.P., died very suddenly on Tuesday night at Pershore. The United Society of Boilermakers has decided, by a large mainity, to secede from the Trades Congress. An inquest held at Lewes on Tuesday on the body of William Thomas Sensier, the jockey who was killed at Plutnoton Steeplechases on Saturday, resulted in a ver- dict of" Accidental death."

OFFENCES AGAINST TEIE FACTORY

ACTS. LOCAL MANUFACITRERS FORD. At the Warrington Borough Police Court, on Friday, James Henry Rogers (Factory Inspeetor) prosecuted in the following cases for offences against the FActory Acts.

William Chantry, confectioner, 43, Horsemarket-At., was summoned for employing on the 24th November, Edward Warburton, Alfred Ashton, and Wm. Ho% les, before six o'clock in the morning.—The Inspecthr said that the three boys were under 18 years of age and com- menced work on the day in question at four o'clock in the morning, contrary to the law. Witness hal visited the place on the 24th and made inquiries. The defendants were liable to a penalty of £5 in each case. Mr. Spencer Wallhead, solicitor, for the defence, said that Mr. Chan- try had do wish to evade the law, and on this matter being reported to him made arrangements for keeping in accordance with the Acts. This being the first offence he pointed out that the penalty was not so great. The Chairman of the Bench (Alderman Harrison) said that in the case of: Hoyles a fine of £1 and costs would be im- posed, and ss. and costs in the other two cases. The Whitecross Co., Limited,were summoned for hav- ing on the 3rd November unlawfully left a flywheel in their possession, unfenced.—Mr. Milner pleaded not ' guilty on behalf of the defendant company.—The Is- Spector said that some twelve months ago the defendants kid built a branch works in Priestlty-.t., and had neg- lected to send him notice, but an Inspector of the De- partment had heard of it and visiting there found that there was- a steam engine which had its flywheel un- fenced. It was very dangerous, and the Inspector caused it to be stopped. The defendants were liable to a penalty of £lO and costs. Assistant-Insp-etir New- lands corroborated Mr. Rogers' statement, and said that he had not in his experience seen a more dangerous wheel. Mr. Rogers himself had afterwards visited the works and seen the wheel, and he corroborated Mr. Newland's statement.—Mr. Milner, for the defence, contended that he was unaware that the engine was un- fenced as they seldom used it. In the meaning of the law the Inspector had classed this part of the works as a separate factory, but Mr. Milner denied that this could be so because there was only a street between the two works,. which were in constant communica- tion. The company were always ready to comply with the demands of the Inspectors, and when Mr. Newlands left he was under the impression that nothing more would be said about it as he had the engine fenced within three hours ef the complaint.—The Bench came to the conclusion that the offence was proved, and infficted a penalty of £2 and casts. Catherine Bancroft, baker, Old-ioad,was charged with , having on the 24th November employed Edward Ban- croft before six o'clock in the morning.—lnspector Rogers said that defendant was summoned for employ- ing the lad from one o'clock in the morning, and he was found at work at five minutes to five. He did not wish to press the case as defendant was in poor circumstances. Defendant stated that she did not know that she was doing wrong is the boy was her own scn.—A. fine of 2t. 6d.and costs only, was imposed.

ANNUAL MEETING,

The seventy-seventh annual meeting of the trustees and managers ot the Warrington Savings Bank was held on Tuesday, at the Bank, Buttermarket-at. A MIRACLE ' AT LEVENSHULME.

PERSONAL INTERVIEW.

THE REPORT FULLY CONFIRMED BY AN EYE WITNESS. A startling incident occurred a short time ago at Levenshulme, Manchester, which caused a great deal of wonderment and set the gossip's tongues going, as to what should be the cause of this event. This was no other than the sudden cure of a man who, for some years, had suffered such terrible and unremitting agony from rheumatism that he had to give up work. Levens- hulme is not a very large place so that the news soon became noised abroad. The patient, a most intelligent 'and widely respected person, is Mr. E. Jackson, residing at Nickerbrook Terrace, Rushford Park, Levenshulme. He is only a young man, but, owing to repeated colds; he contracted rheumatism, and to such an extent was he attacked, that he was told to take his tools home, as he would never have any need for them again. He is at the present time, however, active in his business, and for six months has not been troubled with any of his old pains. This is truly a wonderful pecurrenee, after being three years hors de combat, and , as many reports were current on this interesting matter, the " Examiner " reporter went to see him,and find out thelfacts of the case. Accordingly,one Monday evening, the reporter proceeded to the house, which is in a pretty part of Levenshulme, and overlooking the cricket ground. His knock at the door was answered by Mr. Jackson himself, who appeared brisk, lively, and in gni-41 health. " Well," said Mr. Jackson, in answer to a question, woh at do you want to know about my case ?" "I wish to know, on behalf of the Warrington "Examiner " whether you have really been cured in the miraculous way that is reported." " All right," was the willing answer:and plunging at once into the suhiect, Mr. Jackson said, "My illness arose in the first place from getting one cold after the other. About three years and six months ago I began , agonising rheumatic pains in my knees. , They became worse, and I feared that I could not noes I Ns, attend to my work. I was advised to try the Puxton waters. het still could get no permanent good. My knees were so swollen that I had to cut my trouser and my hands also commenced swelling. I tried the electric ha'tery, hut still found no relief. I tried every remedy vAieh was advertised, but in vain, and I be- came a prispner in the home. THE EXAMINER,

SATURDAY, DECEMBER 22, 1884.

THE owning of tramways seems to Municipal be one of those businesses which Tramways. local authorities can carry on suc- cessfully. If we may form an opinion from articles which appear in the magazines this month, tra.triways prove to be successful investments for a municipality whether worked directly by the servants of the Coporation or through the medium of a Company. Leeds, for instance, mnnicipalised its tramways at the beginning of this year, and already the results have been of a satisfactory character. In spite of the fact that the hours of the conductors have been decreased and their wages increased, there has been a clear profit ef over fifteen hundred pounds. The experiences of another Yorkshire town—Huddersfield—have also been interesting and encouraging. The Corpora- tion of Huddersfield built and equipped its own system of tratnways at a cost of £86,000. The money was „': borrowed for the purpose at three and a half per cent. ; and though the tramway department has to pay six and a quarter per cent. to cover interest and depreciation, the net earnings total up to a sub- stantial amount. In Hudderi-field as in Leeds the Corporation are model employers, as the ernploy6s only work eight hours per day and receive fair wages. In other and larger towns, such as Liver- pool, Manchester, Birmingham and Glasgow the results of municipal ownership are advantageous to the ratepayers, though in some of these towns the working of the trains is undertaken by private companies. In fact, on financial grounds there seems to be no reason whatever why any populous and growing community should be deprived of the many benefits of a system of tramways.

A vivid light is thrown upon the Rescue Work. seething misery of our large

cities, and especially of London, by the fourth annual report in connection with the social scheme of the Salvation Army, which has just been issued. How full of meaning, for instance, is the bare statement that nearly six thousand men and women rosort every night to the Salvationist shelters. These homeless wan- derers vary in age, it is said, front sixteen to eighty-five, and there are amongst them not merely tha waitrel who has scavengered for a precarious existence all his days, but persons of every class and every profession. " The lost son of the peer of the realm lies in the bunk next to the wandering loafer from Covent-garden," says Mr. BRAMWELL BOOTH, "and the ruined lawyer is listenin: to the music, •seated by the side of that brok3u-hearted whose whole life hhs been bringiog him slowly down to this hour." The ease of one member of an old and high-placed family is given in detail, without, of course, any mention of his name. 'He got to selling .news- papers in the streets and further down than that but one night he found social salvation at Black- friar's shelter, .And has now returned to decent society. Mr. BRAMWELL BOOTH, who issues the report in the absence of his father, Provides some useful reading for the new poor-law guardians. It is interesting to note that the farm colony estab- lished by General Boom has boon a sue cess,

financially and otherwise. Upon the agricultural work there is a clear profit, though a rather heavy loss is due to the match factory. The casual ward of the workhouse,in the opinion of Mr. BRAMWELL BOOTH, degrades those who go to it in spite of all that may be done by the administrators of the poor-law. On the other hand it is found that the majority of the men who are degraded by the casual ward will, when transferred to the farm colony, work hard to escape the stigma of pauperism, and in the end are made better, both physically, mentally, and morally.

THE elections within the last few The Guard days have worked something like Election. • a revolution in connection with Boards of Guardians. It cannot exictly be said that a clean sweep has been made of the members of the old Boards, but the changes that have been made are of a sweeping character. This applies, of course with most force to urban districts. The rural, voters have shown less desire for change,and, besides, their choice of candidates has been more limited. Warrington Board of Guardians, among others, will bear the impress of these elections, as only a moderate proportion of the old Guardians will again take their places upon it. There have been changes both in Warrington and in the more outlying parts of the Union. II tydock, for in - stance, has entirely discarded its old representa- tives and sent new men to take their places. It is evident that the voters in this colliery village have not forgotten the strike of last year, and the return of the Congregationalist minister, Mr. RIDETTE, and the Catholic pastor, Father MOORE, for two out of the three wards, is no doubt to some extent an acknowledgment of their active and unselfish services in the relief of distress. In Earlestown and Newton, on the other hand, no change has been made, and the four members who have been re-elected have received the confidence of the new electorate just as they had that of the old. Of the ten Guar- dians returned by Warrington, including Sankey Bridges, six are new to the work they have under- taken, but we venture to think that in each case a change has been made for the better. The return of Mrs. CLAY for Whitecross ward was, as we an- ticipated last week, practically assured. It was , however, not generally expected that her majority would assume such large dimensions. Mr. ROBERT ISHERWOOD, it is true, was not a formidable op- ponent, and he did not improve his chances by a feeble candidature, but at the same time a majority of something like six to one is. a new feature in ward elections in Warrington. Mr. LSHERWOOD can fairly claim one distinc- tion—that of all the candidates who have contested a seat in the local elections of Warring- ton lie has been the most handsomely defeated. That is at least something to have fought for. Mrs. CLAY can also claim that she has established the record majority in connection with the ward elections of the town. With regard to lady candi- datures we regret to notice that MISS BESSIE DUTTON has been defeated in her contest at Moore for a seat on the Runcorn Board of Guardians by that narrowest of narrow majorities—one. Her opponent was the old member for the township and the village publican, and therefore a formid- able rival. There is, however, no finality about a majority of one, and it might easily be more than reversed at the next election. Returning to the Warrington elections, we are not surprised to note that both Mr. W. j. SHAW and Mr. J. HILL, have been ousted from their seats on the Board of Guardians, and we believe they have found useful successors in Mr. CROSS and Mr. LANG. The latter and Mr. MALKIN, who was returned unopposed for Bewsey, will, as th e nominees of the Trades and Labour Coun- cil, be the representatives of the workingmen in a special sense, and they should find a useful sphere of activity in connectim with the administration of poor-law relief. The only regrettable incident ' in connection with the elections is the defeat of the Rev. W. H. DRUMMOND in Fairfield ward. He had a rather powerful opponent in Mr. PILLING an old member of the Board of Guardians on whose behalf the most vigorous exertions were made. Mr. DRUIKMOND fought on strictly inde- pendent lines, but, in spite of the agreement or understanding to the contrary, some of the Con- servative officials did not spare themselves in the support of Mr. PILLING.

HENRY MILLING & CO. LTD. MERCHANTS, WARRING roN.

LOCAL NEWS.

PROPOSED TREAT To RUNCORN POOR CHILDREN. A worthy effort is being made by the Rev. r. Gastaldi to provide ft hot-pot dinner for poor children, irrespective of creed, on Christmas Day at Sr. Edward's School. Tickets for such are to be obtained from Mr. Percival at the Town Hall. Those desirous of subscribing to the fund will find several places mentioned in an advertisement in another column where subscriptions can be received.

PRESENTATION AT RUNCORN.. Miss Ainsworth. who has occupied the position of superintendent of the girls' Sunday school at St. Michael's, Runcorn, for a number of years, has been presented with a lady's handsome hand-bag and photographic album by the teachers and scholars. Mr. E. R,idge, treasurer of the school, presided over the proceedings. Miss Stubbs exolained the object of the meeting, and Mr. E. Pownall made the pre- sentation. Miss Ainsworth suitably acknowledged the gift, and thanked the teachers for their kind- ness to her. adopted.

RUNCORN CONGREGATIONAL CHURCH.—GRAND LITERARY AND MUSICAL TREAT. On behalf of the above church funds a highly satisfactory and strikingly attractive evening of oratory music and song was given on Monday by that extraordinary versatile elocutionist,author,and musician, Mr. Powell Thomas (London), who is well-known here. His two hours programme of poetic. dramatic recitals, songs, instrumental melange, literary sketches, and lecturette, was of reinarkable brilliancy, and sufficed to show the wondertul range of this gentleman's marvellous talents, and was again more than ordinarily suc- cessful, and held the audience captive. His rendi- tion of " The Bells" (E. A. Poe) was nothing short of a revelation, no item in the whole programme was more enjoyable than the grand poetic and dramatic rendering of this. All the other selections received a grand interpretation, and were given with a truthfulness, pathos, and a reality truly won- derful, and gained a new charm as conveyed by this consummate artist. The vocal and instrumental selections were also presented with the utmost taste, artistic finish,and spirit, rousing the audience to genuine enthusiasm. The entertainment was quite a literary, intellectual, and musical event. COLLIER'S sell everything for the smallest possible profits. Advt CAMDEN WESLEYAN CHURCH SALE OF WORK. Kennan, assistant secretary; and the following committee: Miss Ctayden, Miss 0. Stubbs, Mr. G. S. Smith. Mr. Phillip, and Mr. Ros- ser. PAINFUL INCWENT AT AN INQUEST AT RUNCORN. Mr. J. E. Worsley held an inquest at Runcorn Police Court, on Saturday morning, relative to the death of Frances Sherratt, wife of G. E. Sherratt, 6, Stanley-at., who died on Thursday.—Dr. Robinson stated that he saw the deceased for the first time on Wednesday morning. She was in a condition verging on collapse, and suffering from pain and sickness. He tried to get her warm by giving her brandy and outward applications. The deceased's friends appeared to have done what they could for her. He gave her some opium to relieve the pain. The deceased attributed her illness to eating some fish on Sunday night, and her symptoms were con- sistent with her having eaten tainted fish ; although they were not peculiar to one condition. She died on the morning following his visit. The d;cesused was not at the Stores with her husband on Tuesday evening. He made a post-mortem examination of the body, and he had found a perforating ulcer in her stomach, from which the liquid contents of the stomach had passed into the general cavity of the abdomen. The collapse resulting from that had been the cause of death. He had learned that. the deceased had been ailing for three or four years and had suffered much from pain in the stomach. She must have had a life of miisery. -- Her husband was called but fainted, and was afterwards seized by a fit. lie had to be carried out of court. Margaret Hughes, 16, Milton-road, Widnes, said the deceased was her daughter. She was married a week ago, and was 23 years of age. She hai suffered much from what they considered was indigestion. Three years ago she came home from a situation in Liverpool suffer- ing from a pain which she attributed to having strained herself lifting some furniture. They had not, however, felt any cause for alarm.—The Coroner said this was a very sad inquiry indeed, and he was sure the jury would agree with him that they felt. the greatest sorrow for her husband in the very sad loss he hal sustained, and the very sad circum- stances attending its—A verdict of death from " natural causes" was returned. On Tuesday afternoon Osaut 4.30, five men em- ployed on the Ship Canal; and to others who had been out on a shooting expedition, entered a boat on the Mersey side of the Ship Canal for the pur- pose of crossing to the Weston Point side. They had proceeded a short distance when the wash of the " Emulator " which was towing a pontoon, cause.l the small boat to come into contact with the latter, and completely overturned the small craft with its occupants. Three of the men succeeded grasping the towing rope attached to the "Emu- lator," and held on to it until rescued. Two others jumped on the barge, but a man named Wakefield of Clarence Terrace, Weston, and Samuel Cosper, lock man, were not so fortunate. The former went under the pontoon and in consequence of the runn- ing out of the tide was carried by the strong cur- rent adistance of some 200 yards. Fortunately the crew of the boat " Virginia " saw him in the water and succeeded in rescuing him. He was in a very exhausted condition and suffering from injury to his chest through coming in contact with the pon- toon. Cooper stuck to the upturned boat, which afterwards righted itself, and iloa.;ed to the Wet- ton Point side, where he was rescued. BANKRUPTCY OF A RUNCORN TRADESMAN.

Samuel Peter Lewis, 53, Devonshire-square, Run- corn, painter and decorator, appeared to undergo his public examination at the Warrington Bank- ruptcy Court on Friday last week, before Mr. Regis- trar Ridgway. The gross liabilities amounted to 2245 4s. 4d., and the assets £22 2s. 10d. The bank- rupt alleged as the cause of his failure, bad trade and competition. He stated that he had been in business as a painter and decorator for the last seven years having commenced with a capital of £8 only. He alleged that not having kept proper hooks of account, he 'was unable to explain in detail how his deficiency had arisen except as to £6 is. id. had debts, and £5 sheriff's charges. He admitted that he was aware of his insolvency at the end of 1893, and that the bulk of his liabilities had been contracted with such knowledge. The bankrupt further stated, that his wife was tenant of the dwelling-house, 16, York-st., Runcorn, in which he now resided, and that the furniture belonged to her. —After a short examination by Mr. A. B. Potter, the assistant official receiver, the examination was closed.

THE URBAN COUNCIL ELEC- TION AT RUN CORN. THE RESULT OF THE POLLING.

The polling on Saturday for tte election of eighteen Urban district councillors for Runcorn, passed off very quietly, considering the great interest taken in the elec- tion. Only one ward was allowed to go uncontested, and this was Mersey Ward in which Messrs. Once, Posnett and Kirkham were returned unopposed. In the five remaining wards, keen contests took place—perhaps the tamest fight being that in Heath ward where Mr. Hayes challenged the three rairing Commissioners, Messrs. P. Speakman, J.P., C.C., J. W. Lightburn, and J. Pritchard. Additional placards were posted on the hoardings on Saturday morning from which it would appear that the workingman has many friends, and this was most noticeable in Bridgewater iVard, where no fewer than four candidates announced themselves as the workingman's friend." Mr. F. A. Lake, the return- ing officer, had made complete arrangements for the poll and at 12 noon precisely the polling booths were apened. They remained so until eight p.m.,when the ballot boxes were conveyed to the Town Hail and remained there until Monday morning at ten o'clock when the counting commenced. Appended are the results. The first three candidates 2iven in each ward are elected, and their period of office—one, two, or three years—is determined ty the position they hold in the ballot.

THE DISTRESS IN RUNCORN. MEETING OF THE RELIEF COMMITTEE.

The distress in Runcorn and district is now becoming acute, consequent upon the continued stoppage of several work-4, and there is the prospect of a gloomy , Christmas-time for numbers of poor people in the town and district. It is pleasing to note that efforts are being made to relieve the distress, and the Relief Committee which has been formed met on Tuesday evening in the Town Hall, when Dr. McDougall presided. There were also present: Phe Rev. Canon Maitland Wood, Rev. T. Brackenbury, Rev. H. B. Frith, J. Olding and A. Bartram, Messrs. J. B. Holland, J. W. Lightburn, T. Salkeld, S. Pope. E. Imison, A. R. Norman, Wm. S. Calionder, F. J. Norman, J.P., J. Poole, R. H. Posnett, J. Wilson, Rhodes, and Worthington. A report of the sub-committee to the effect that employers of labour, and managers of works be authoriztd to distr;bute relief to deserving men connected with their establish- ments' pending further action, was adopted. With regard to cases outside these, a sub-committee was ap- pointed to prepare plans which would be submitted to an adjourned meeting to be held on Friday. It was decided to have eight collecting boxes in various parts of the town for the receipt of donations. The sums received amounted to £lO, £5 from Mr. Burton and a like amount from Messrs. Win. R. Hartley and Company per Mr. Walter Collier. The following sub- scriptions have been promised: Dr. Steele £5, Highfield Tannery £5, William Rowland £5. The weekly contri- butions promised were : employees of the United Alkali Comp Any at the Runcorn soapery £4, United Alkali Company £5. employees at the Old Quay Works £3, F. J. Norman £2, and Dr. McDoutrall, Charles Timmins, Canon Wood, S. Taylor, J. Brundrit, P. Speakman, and J. W. Lightburn £1 each. The Co-operative Society has promised to give 400 four-lbs. loaves each week.

THE RUNCORN MUSICAL SOCIETY'S CONCERT.

On Monday evening the Runcorn Musical Sacietv— assisted by a few Warrington friends—gave the secOnd concert of the season, consisting of the performance of Mendelssohn's " Walpurgis Night "and a miscellaneous programme. It is gratifying to note that the two per- formances ventured upon, have, from a musical point of view, been most successful, and Mr. Crossley, the capable conductor of the Society, is to be congratulated upon the result. The attendance on Monday evening was not so large as could be wished for,but the perform- ance was a pronounced success, and the applause of the audience attested its appreciation. Mendelssolin's cantata, the first Walpurgis night, or the eve of the first of May (a poem by Goethe), is full of interesting matter, dealing, As it does, with the rites and sacrifices of the ancient Druids. It is a piece bristling with difficul passages, and a fiae example of dramatic cantatat Commencing with an overture in A miner, descriptive of the stormy weather directly preceding spring, and leading on to a delightful tenor solo (a Druid) with chorus, interrupted by the warning voice of a knowing woman of the people, whose burden is taken up by the chorus. A magnificent bass solo, followed by a charac- teristic and masterly appeal to the Druid guards, and taken up by them, prepares the fanciful and picturesque chorus of guards and the people—(" Come with torches,' —perhaps the most original of Mendelssohn's vocal pieces. The Christians express their terror and flight in another ensemble, and a grand, noble invocation, entrusted to the has, and accompanied by the chorus, ends worthily this admirable composition, which was first performed in England at the Philharmonic Concert of the Bth July, 1844, under the com- poser's personal direction. The leading vocalists were Miss Alice Walker (contralto), Mr. Charles Blaghnro (tenor), and Mr. Robert Grice (bass), a trio why, proved themselves equal to the exigencies of the occasion. The chorus acquitted itself admirably throughout, time and tune being most satisfactory, One number worthy of mention, in which the chorus shone, was, "Come with torches." A well equipped orchestra coped with the difficulties of the overture, and the several choruses. The second part of the programme was of a very miscellaneous character, including solos for each of the principal vocalists, three dances and an andante "The Chimes" for the orchestra, and also a choral fantasia on National airs for chorus and orchestra Miss Alice Walker's first solo was " Nobil Signor " (Huguenots) by Meyerbeer, hut she was far more fitic- cessful in her next effort " Slumber Song " (Heins) in which her rich contralto voice was heard to advantage. In this song she was accompanied on the violin by Mr. J. W. Collinson. An encore was demanded, and Miss Walker complied by the singing of an ad- ditional verse. Mr. Charles Blagboro is a tenor sinew. of note, and he treated the aedience to an ad- mirable interpretation of the pleasing song " Mary," the niquant nsints of the story being made the most of by the vocalist. Needleas to say, he was vocifernusl? recalled. Mr. Robert Grice was equally successful in the song" The Old Soldier." which was well suited to his fine bass voice. The song. a lthotieh an old soldier's wail, is nevertheless, tinged with humour, and the vivanitr with which Mr. Grire sang it, showed that he had fully caught the snirit of the words. In the three dances—Morris dance—the orchestra provided a musical treat, and the loud and continued applause which greeted the termination of each piece was evidence of the appreciation of the audience. A most finished per- formance of " The Chimes," was also given. But the choral fantasia on National airs by the chorus and arehestra was undoubtedly the gem of the evening. In all, ten Notional airs were even, and the swing and Villa-ire of the parts were all that could be desired. Mr. Frank H. Crossley ably piloted all concerned through the ntimerous difficulties, and the performance was a credit, alike for the •conductor and themselves.

RUNCORN WESLF,TAN CIRCUIT.- QUARTERLY MEETING.

Under the presidency of the Rev. T. Brackenbury, the quarterly meetingof the Raricorn Wesleyan circuit was held in Brunswick vestry on Monday evening. The Revs. W George and W. Lile Stone, and representatives from different parts of the circuit,were also present. Prior to the commencement of the business, the chairman ex- pressed the sympathy of the meeting with his colleague, the Rev. W. Lile Stone, whose medical attendants had recommended him to take a prolonged rest for the benefit of his health. Be (the speaker) hoped that the enforced retirement from astive labour would restore him to per- fect health. Tha financial atatement of the circuit stewards showed a slight adverse balance. This was accounted for by the fact that the quarter had been a short one. The Chairman submitted his numerical report showing an increase of membership. This was considered satisfactory. A vote of thanks was accorded to Mr. E. Taylor. whose term of office as circuit steward has ex- pired, for the efficient manner in which he had fulfilled his duties. A similar compliment was giverrto Mr. R. Rawlinson, who had acted as Mr. Tarinr's colleague iuring the past twelve months.—Mr. F. J. Nnrman,J.P., was elected steward. Thanks were tendered to Messrs. RUNCORN FicEE LIBRARY COMMITTEE.

TFIE LAST MEETING.

At the Town Hall, on. Tuesday evening, the last meet ing of the Runcorn Free Library Corn mit tee was held Mr. W. Handley occupied the chair, and there were ale present :—Messrs. T. Riley. S. Pope, and J. D. Jones (librarian). From the monthly report submitted it appeared that the number of books issued from the lend- ing department was 1,882, distributed as follows : ' Theology 31, philosophy 10. science 72, history, etc. 154. poetry 7, fiction 1,447, and miscellaneous 161. This waS an increase of 52 on the corresponding period last year. In the reference department there had be( n 46 volumes , issued, an increase of two.—Mr. Pope said that as that was their last meeting he wished to move that the best thanks be given to Mr. Handley for his conduct in the chair during the year. He (the speaker) had only been a member of the committee during the past 12 months, and it had been a pleasure to him to see the way in which the Chairman performed his duties. He re-echoed tb wish expressed by Mr. Handley, namely that he would be found associated with the conducting of the free library under the new order of things, and he (Mr. Pope) I would be glad to serve under him. The speaker then proceeded to refer to the great interest taken by Mr. Handley in the free library SiRCO its inception.—Mr. Riley seconded the motion, remarking that the com- mittee had tried to cater for all classes of the reading public. They had recently adopted a section which would be doubly interesting to the musically inclined. Another advantageous means of promoting the success of the library had been instituted as a result of thEit librarian's visit to Ireland, vtz. : "Half-hour chats on books and authors." The motion having been carried, Mr. Handley responded, saying that if there was any work he had loved, it was that connected with the Free Library, whether as a member or chairman. He had felt in his place th,re, and he had no doubt they would find themselves engaged in the same labour under the new conditions. He thought all the Comnaissioners had very kindly thoughts of the Library Committee and ita work, and there was every indication that the new Council would do all it could to make the library more successful in the future than in the past. They had got to a stage when they could consider it one of the per- manent institutions of the town. They had got books of reference that would last for a very long period. It. had been a pleasure to hirn to perform the duties of chairman, and he was delighted to find that his own conduct bad proved acceptable.—This was all the busi- ness of public interest.

THINGS HEARD AND SEEN AT I i FicCORN. •

BY OUR RUNCORN CORRESPONDENT.

Runcorn has just emerged from one of the most keenly contested elections for local honours which have occurred in the town. Toe elect ion of Urban district councillors and poor law guardians was taken simultaneously and although the latter was almost eclipsed by the fight for the honour of dis- trict councillor, there were, nevertheless, some interesting results. A noteworthy feature was the defeat of Mr. Jone, a retiring guardian, in each of the five wards he contested, and altiou,gh it was understood that he threw the gauntlet down io Norman in Bridgewater Ward, this latter gentleman won by a handsome majority. The fact of the matter is, Mr. Jones had ton many strings to his bow, and it is unfortunate for him that he did not retire from some of the wards on the withdrawal day. The new representative on the Board, for Runcorn, is Canon Maitland Wood, who will, no doubt, prove a capable member.

The Liberal Council infused much life into the District Council ,on test by the issue of the well- known circular, and they now have the gratifica- tion of seeing some of tneir adopted candidates elected on the new Council. If a tithe of the pro- gressive programme is carried into effect some remarkable changes in local affairs may be looked, forward to. Of the eighteen candidates returned, one half of them did not sit on the old Board so that there is a marked addition of "new blood" ‘,13 the council. The shipwrielta claim to have id- creased their representatives by two.

It is said of the tailor, and perhaps there is a modicum of truth in the statement that he changes every man's coat but his own and it may perhaps be said of the pressman that he ventilates almost everybody's grievances but his own. There is one matter which deserves to be brought under the notice of our newly-fledged councillors and that is the accommodation given to the press when the authority resolves itself into committee or on the occasion of other private transactions. The re- porters are generally asked to retire from the Boardroom but when this does occur the only accommodation at present provided is stand- ing-room in that ever draughty passage at the Town Hall. A most delightful place to wait—perhaps for an hour! The, unfor- tunate reporter is soon possessed of cold feet, and to attempt to write under such unfavourable cir- cumstances would be a most difficult and uncom- fortable task. Pehaps under the new regime better accommodation will be provided.

The contests in the rural parishes in the Runcorrb Union for Rural councillors and Par?sh councillorn were not devoid of interest. At Newton-by-Dares- bury, a mere hamlet, with about two dozen electors, the whole machinery for a contested election had to be put in motion for the election of a rural councillor. The retiring Guardian, Mr. E. Bretherton, was opposed by Mr. John W. Taylor, a signalman. Mr. Bretherton maintained his seat, but only by 13 votes to 8. At Whitley thirty persons went to the poll, and when the votes were counted it was found that 15 votes had been recorded for each candidate. In Ntsore, Mr. J. L.. Jones, publican, maintained his seat by the narrow majority of one vote. This contest created especial intere,t from the tact thut it was the only election throughout the Union where a lady was the oppos- ing candidate. Miss Bessie Dutton, his opponent, was, however successful in securing a sea on the Parish Council.

Warrington journeyed to Manchester on Saturday and had no difficulty in defeating their opponents. The game, however, was not of a very one sided character, though we imaeine Warrington could have piled on a bigger score hail it been necessary. But from the first, 'it was evident that Warrington was the stronger team. They passed remarkably well, James Bate and Nevins in particular being conspicuous for well timed and accurate passing, of which Barber and Burton seldom failed to take advantage, The forwards, too, played a grand game, showing themselves a much superior lot te their opponents. Jolley and Buckley were the only absentees from the Warrington team, their places being taken by Houghton and Savage. It was said that seven of the chosen Manchester fifteen failed to put in an appearance. THE ANGELS' SONG.

Still o'er Ephratah's lonely hills The stars of heaven serenely glow; While midnight's gloomy shadow fills The silent vales far down below.

There shepherds keep their watch by night As lowly shenherds watch'd of old; To guard from danger and affright, The peaceful flock within the fold. ,•'.• But o'er those hills and valleys fair, - No heavenly vision meets their eye; No angels break the silence there, • With wondrous tidings from the sky. • Yet, though the song is heard no more That fill'd the starry heights sublime ; Its echos wing from shore to shore, Coeval with the flight of time. i•;‘) 0 yes, they aaft o'er land and sea, 8- . O'er fruitfuTfields and deserts wid That weary men, where'er they be, May list their tones .of mercy mild. But slow are mortals to discern The music as it floats along ; And all unwilling still to learn, The import of the Angels' song. ' Their selfish views of human life, And eager rush for sordid gain ; With cries of sorrow, want and strife, Alas, shut out the heavenly strain. An o, as or, the rolling years, Rear Acllufs children to the grave ; Mor% hont-t.A4 still the world appears, More impotent sits schemes to save. 0 Thou whose birth in David's town, The angels sang with joy supreme ; -Thine was the Crr' iss be Thine the Crown, That fill'd the Hebrew prophets' dream. In this, earth's darkest, saddest hour, Let not Thy chariot wheels delay ; But come in all thy kingly power, And wide thy peaceful sceptre sway. A PASTORAL EPIC ON WARRINGTON,

Oh ! twould be a pleasure T. ma b'yond maisure, The charms of Warrington to proudly sing— In rhyme and metre

Sublime, and sweeter Than °zones the Mersey round us here cloth fling.

We've streets so narrow, 'Bout fit for a barrow, In the central parts of our important town;

, And tortuous alleys, From out which sallies The bulky bus, to ride pent-up folks down !

We've smells vagarious, Profound and various, From the slum's dark reeking;, rich in every To sewage extruiions, Tan works' effusions, And the polyglot odorines around Pig-Hill ! We've the grandest gates, sir, • And the highest rates,cir, • ' Of any town in Btitain's round you'll meet ; While on Cother side, sir— To check our pride, sir— We've the worst arrangement, faith ! of slum and stret This combination Of ostentation, And tawdry waste—with civic squalor deep, - Resembles greatly The sight so stately, Of a white silk top-hat on a working sweep! We've smoke in volutn-s, And prodigious columns, To decorate our scenery at large ; Our children's faces And hands it graces With artistic traceries, all free of charge! It's suffocating And irradiating, It penetrates the innermost recess ; Clothes, cuff; and collars • It astutely " follers," And with Nubian pencilling; d,ith them impress. What a thund-ring pity ' T were, from this ditty, To omit the sweet complexioned Sankey Brook, 7 And aromas luscious That so freely flush us, : As by its bonny banks we " take our hook " ! Had I the genius, Of the Pope or Venus, Who conquered CTsar at fair Waterloo, How proud I'd lay on Both oil and crayon,. In writing epics, Warrington, on y( u ! (To be. Continue .ne,it ('entury.)
