Rose Book 22, 12, '94..

THE LATE INSPECTOR SNELLING. A service in memory of Ihe death of District In.. spector Snelling. of the T 4. St N.W. Railway, was preached at St. Barnaba.' Church, on Sunday after- noon, by the Vicar, the Rev. G. Allton. A proces- sion was formed at half-past three, at Bank Quay Station, and marched to St Barnabas' Church, headed by the London and North-Western traffic band, from Garston Dock. The procession was headed by Mr. Starkey (Warrington stationmaster): Mr. Peters, stationraaste,r at Wigan and late of War- rington; Mr. Hatter, night stationmaster, Warring- ton; the Dock Superintendent from Garston ; Inspectors Brookshaw, Wigan District, and Carter, Widnes District, Mr. Warren, Latchford station- master, Mr. Scragg. Broarlheath stationmaster, and the stationmaster from Heatley, traffic inspectors. Turton and Blayden, and other members of the various grades, including a body of signalmen who worked under the supervision of the deceased. About 140 persons with the widow and children, attended the service, which was conducted by the Rev. G. Allton. A collection was made on behalf of the subscription fund for the widow and childrn of the deceased. The procession returned at 515, by way of Bewsey-road and Crosfield-st., to Bank Quay, where the people dispersed to return to their respective towns. The whole of the arrangements were carried out in an excellent manner under the supervision and general arrangements of Mr. S;ar- key, Warrington stationmaster. t• , ' ' . ,•.

WARRINGTON A V. MANCHESTER A.—This was the first aleeting of these two well-known clubs for two seasons. Vane:ester should have visited Warrington at the close of last year, hut owing to the frost the match was cancelled. When lined upon the field on Saturday, War- rington were without the services of Cunningham, Houghton. Savage, Hill and Burrows, while the visitors were without H. Bright. Notwithstanding the great change Warrington were able to put a heavy score on. Manchester winning the toss, Warrington elected to play towards the pavilion goal. A good nureber of spec- tators had gathered when the ball was set in motion. warrington showed eagerness to have a good game, and had the ball shortly after the kick off in the visitors' , quarters. A scrimmage took place near the god line. which resulted in Lawless getting possession. 'Dodging through, he was collared near the line when on the point of scorine. Another scrimmage was the result, this one enabling Bennett to :et possession, and dropping down "he scored. The goal kick failed. After the re-start ' Manchester were penalised for off-side play and a free kick granted to Warrington, with mothing resulting. Neat passing was now the main feature of the game. Prom a pass from ono of the Warrington forwards A. P. Hoegone of the visitors' three-quarterseiecured the ball, and dribbling it the length of the field scored a try amid the enthusiastic cheers of the spectators. A number of ,scrimmages and line outs took place up to half-time. When kalf-time was called, the score read, Warrington 1 try, Manchester 1 try. On re-starting the visitors proved to be dangerous but could not add anything to their scere. Warrington now seemed to be in better form. Hardly had the game been going when Lawless secured, and after a brilliant run scored, the goal kick being of no value: After re-commencing, War- rine/ten still pressed their opponents who,however,trans- ferred to the home territory. The transference was of a short duration. A short interval had elapsed when Hughes got possession. and running a short distance, succeeded in placing the ball over the goal line. Forster kicked for goal but could not improve the score. Man- chester were again penalised for off-side play, and a free kick granted to Warrington, thus enabling the bomesters .to score another try. Shortly after this Swift took possession and scored,Randles neatly coevert- ing. After a short bout sif give and take play, Thornton seeing an opportunity afforded to him got possession and scored. A few minutes before time was called Lawless again scored. The final score was Warringten. 2 goals, 5 tries ; Manchester, 1 try. The players were—Warring- ton Mannion, full back; Appleton, Lawless, Edlesten and Forster, three-quarter backs ; Clark and Bennett, half-backs ; Makin, Rendles, Hughes, Knight, Sanders, Swift, Thornton and Dodson, ferwards. Manchester: H. Vichoff, full back; A. P. Hogg, T. Whittingham, B. R. Bliss and J. G. Peal, three-quarter backs ;C. Re Pilkineton and H. M. Milnes. half-backs ; C. Hempen, J. MeMonies, C. F. Barrett, A. S. Kidd, G. P. Mandley, McConnell and S. Paylor, forwards. WARRINGTON CASTLE RIIBBER WORKS v. RICHMOND AND Co.—At Wildersnool. Lally started for the Castle, Rooney returning. Give and take play ruled during the remainder of the first half,at the close of which nothing had been scored. In the second half the play of the Castle forwards fell away, and Richmond's had a fair share of this half being very dangerous on more than one occasion, the back play of the Castle also lacked that combination so essential to success being very erratic at times whilst Richmond's improved as the game pro- gressed, hut though they strove hard they could not score. The end of a pleasant game being an even draw. Castle Rubbers nil. Richmond's nil.

tetter4 to the Obttor.

Sir,—Reading in your issue of a fortnight ago the totter on Pasteuri.m and hydrophobia, in which your Correspondent endeavours to make it appear that M. Pasteur's inoculations are both a delusion and a snare, will you permit me to state that they are neither, and th4t M. Pasteur is one of the most careful, accurate and greatest of living scientists ; in fact he is a great im- mortal benefactor of mankind ? Many years ay., ten persons were bitten by a rabid dog, in St. Helens, and I requested the father of one of the bitten persons to have a post-mortem examination made and a portion of the madulla forwarded to Dr. Barrow, of Liverpool, and also to Profeseor Florslev, of London, and that those bitten should he at once sent to Paris to be treated. This was done, and it was proved that the dog.was suffering from rabies when those persons were bitten, and though was,' years have elapsed not one of them has died of hydrophobia, and it is almost certain that some of thorn Would have done so ere this had they not undergone the preventive treatment. The statement that 268 persons have died from hydrophobia after undergoing the pre- ventive treatment is not correct. Your correspondent states that deaths from hydropho- bia have increased in France. If we consider the large climber of parsons who have come from Russia after being fearfully bitten about the head and face by rabid wolves, and that the incubation period of hitea about the head and face is nearly always short, and though Such cases were nearly boneless, vet it -vas still the duty of M. ===== 0002601_18941222_art0137.txt | included=true | included =====
wheat fall into the ground and die,it abideth alone: but if it die it bringeth forth much fruit." This then is the text which Mr. Peers has contradicted again and again, and in order to get facts he contradicts it, alsc he has, as I said, been experimenting. Now, what I want to point out is that the experiment bore no relationship to the text it was designed to re- fute. Now, in order that the seed should die and the plant to live and to bring forth much fruit, there was a condition stated in the text which was that the seed should first fall ire-o the ground. Did J. P. comply with this condition ? Not he! He cut the life out of the seed with a pen knife and then put it on a damp cloth, and the dead lifeless seed he put away, and like Queen Anne it has been dead ever since. Had he (Mr. Peers) really wished to prove the truth Of the text he would have complied with its conditions by letting the seed in its integrity fall into the ground— instead of which he put a fraction of the seed on a damp cloth furnished with suear. (See his 'e-ter of last Saturday)—, How in the name of:common sens could such a silly freak prove the truth or falsity of the text? Let us consider for a moment what would happen if the seed did not die. Why, it would continue to throw out new plants. Animals do not expend the whole of their living resources in giving birth to their first young, and as a consequence they continue to reproduce their kind. But we never knew of wheat seed having a suc- cession of plants, because the whole of its life, its ener- gies and substance, except its integument, are all ex- pended at its first germination. How often do we hear of the birth of the first child killing the mother? too often ! And this is the case of the wheat seed. If it did not part wi:hits life the plant could not possess it, if it did net part with its substance, the plant could have no visible means of existence. Therefore, it dies and " bringeth forth much fruit." Any person can have most undeniable proof of this by pulling up a well- developed wheat plant. • I have done so and found only the outside shell of the seed, and this was split in all its sides. Death is one of Nat • Irst-class agents. Were it not for death, nature , ' not escape bankruptcy. For instance, what sh)uld we have done for coal had vegetation not died? During the existence of the wheat plant three deaths occur—the sued dies at its first ger- mination, the blossom lives for a short time,and as soon as its work of love is complete it dies, and, lastly, when the seed in the ear is perfected the stalk with its leaves dies also. This, then, is the gospel of life and death.— .. Yours truly,

FELIX SUMMERS,

CHILDRENS' CONCEIT AT WARGRAVE Mr. Chandley, head master, of the Wargrave day schoo's, made his first venture as a purveyor of public amusement, on Thursday evening, by giving with the assistance of his scholars, a children's concert and en- tertainment in the Wargrave school. A few years ago the juvenile concerts given by the children of Wargrave school were always looked forward to as being sure to afford a pleasant evening. It has fallen to the good fortune of Mr. Chandley to revive these acceptable en- tertainments, which not only conduce to the enjoyment of adults, but afford the means of instruction to the children not otherwise obtainable. Of course, such re- presentations cannot be inaugurated without assistance, and this Mr. Chandley received from Mr. Newton (Assistant Master), Miss Ratcliffe (Head Mistres.), and tteother teachers, and their labours must have been of an arduous character, judeing from the proficiency to which the youngsters attained. The schoolroom had been slightly altered for the occasion, the partition was taken down by Mr. Slee, who also erected the capacious platform to accommodate the large number takisg part. The speeial costumes used in some of the representations were made by the teachers with assistance from the Misses Carson. Mrs. W. Thompson, jun.. and other friends. The entertainment was in aid of the school funds, and the room was filled with a very appreciative audience,which heartily applauded the vari- ous items produced. Opening with a pianoforte solo "Home, sweet Home„' cleverly executed by Master A. Purnell. the programme was proceeded with in a spirited manner. Passing °vex the choral pieces, which were admirable sung by a choir of about 50 senior boys and girls, and includins "The dear homeland," "The Lindener Duke of Plaza Toro." "The King's Own," and" Clleletmas Bells," we shall go on to what we may term the " get " items. First is the • song "Private Tommy Atkins," which was excellently sung by Masters Tarbuek, Bradshaw, and Wrigley,in costume. The next item was a " fan drill," by junior boys and girls under the guidance of Miss Ratcliffe, and they succeeded in winning the hearty applause of the audience. Master Wrigley, attired as a professor, gained a recall for the song" The Professor and L.L.D." Two little mites capitally recited "Dolly and the Doctor," Ada Raver taking the part of Dolly's gar-Ilan, and Herbert Payne, that of the doctor. Single stick drill by boys in gymnas- tic costume was very cleverly executed under the direc- tien of Mr. Newton. two of the boys afterwards giving an illustration of fencing. A nautical song in costume" Three jolly sailors," by Masters H. Setton,H. Flamblett,and Walter Creeciswas redemanded, and the last verse was repeated. The second part onened with the piano solo. "Sounds celestial," by Miss M. Sweetlove. and this was followed by a prettily- executed scarf drill, by the junior boys and girls. Tne next item was a song and dance in costume, " Nursery Rhyme'." by eight junior boys and girls. The boys were attired as courtiers, with sash and sword, and the girls as maid., something after the style shown in the nursery ryhme pictures. Whilst the elder ,oys and girls sang the rhymes, the youngsters executed the quadrillee in an excellent manner. A Highland dance by twelve senior boys in kilts and tartan, was very cleverly performed, and they received a deserved round of applause. A recitation, "Mother's Pocket," by Alice Sankey and Eliza Poulson, was followed by bar-nell exercise by six- teen boys and girls to musical acconspaniment. Attired AR an old man. Master A. Tarbuck brought down the house by the really excellent manner in which he sang, "I love to think of the days when I was young." Coupled with a good voice, young Tarbuck possessee plenty of assurance, and he fully deserved the enthu- siastic encore he received. The ilext item was a wax- works of real moving fieures, Mastsr H. Bradshaw officiating as showman, and a most excellent showman h. made, his descriptive lecture in connection with the various figures being most amusing. The programme was most fittingly terminated by the carol " Christmas Bells." At the conclusion a vote of thanks to Mr.Cband- - Icy and his assistants was moved by Mr. Borron and heartily accorded.

Let them realise in their heart of hearts the relation in which they stood to each and every man, and they would have one of the mest powerful weapons God had given them. When they saw anything which was wrong, it was their duty to set themselves against it and to endeavour to sten it. Let them put their whole soul into the work. About six o'clock on Monday morning Mrs. Pen- nington, wife of Mr. W. F.Pennington, postmaster, of Lord-st., Croft, hearing a noise in the office be- low awoke her husband, who went into the office to see what was the matter. lie found that the cash drawer had been broken open, and the largest part of its contents taken out. On going to: the door he saw two persons running away, but ea ac- count of the darkness could not give a description of them. Information was at once given to P.C. Callaghan, but no clue to the thieves has yet been obtained.

NEWTON DISTRICT COUNCIL

ELECTIONS.

NEWTON-IN-MA.KERFIELD. The polling for the election of 15 councillors for the Newton-in-Makerfield Urban District Council, opened on Monday at 12 o'clock, at the various booths. Consider- able interest was evinced in the election, and the walls in various parts of the township were plentifally adorned with the usual election literature. The day was of a miserable character, and as a consequence the vicinity of the polling stations was practically deserted, until about six o'clock in the evening. The elements some- what cleared about this time, and workmen being then at liberty they mustered in considerable numbers round the booths. Vehicles were freely used for conveying dilatory voters to the poll, and as the time approached for the cessation of voting, the streets wore a busy 1 aspect. In the Wargrave and Newton wards the voters were counted at the respective polling stations, but those for Crow-lane, Town Hall and Viaduct were counted at the Town Hall, and the result declared in the latter case abogt 11 o'clock. At Wargrave the result was known about 9 o'clock, and at Newton shortly after 10 o'clock. Outside the town Ball, Earlestewn, a large crowd assemeled and waited patiently for some two or three hours for the results, amusing themselves by cheering and hosting the various candidates. The police arrange- ments were in charge of Inspector Morgan,but the crowd was of an orderly character, and gave the pGlice no treuble whatever. Below will be found the results of the voting in the various wards : • WARGRAVH. The poll was declared about nine o'clock by Mr. R. Chandley, deputy returning officer, as follows :—*Col- lingwood 215, Slee 171, Thompson 153, Mouncey 129, Davies 122, Pryor 94. Guardians: Bent 224, Uns worth 75. It gave him great pleasure to address them as the head of the pOll ; he did not expect it, as he had never, before been elected by th D ratepayers. Now he felt a different man altogether, he had got them with him, amd they could rest assured that their intereat would he very well looked after. (Cheers.)—Mr. J. Slee and Mr. W. Thonapsoe, Jun , also thank,cl the electors for returning them to the Council.—Mr. E. L. Bent also returned thanks for the honour they had conferred upon him by returning him as Guardian.

THE EXAM - NER. SATUR I)A Y,

DECEMBER 22, 1894.

NEWTON BOARD OF COMMISSIONERS The final meeting of the above Board was held on Thursday at the Town Hall, Earlestown. Mr. .J. W. Emmett presided, and there were also present Messrs. T. Ormand, E. T. Evans, P. McMahon, W. Collingwood, G. Ratcliffe, J. E. Worsley (law clerk), and R. Brierley (clerk and surveyor). LETTER FROM COLONEL MCCORQUODALE. The Chairman read the following letter from Colonel McCorquociale. " Dec. 19, 1894, Dear Sir,—Not being able to attend to-morrow's final meeting of the Newton Commissioners, I write to thank you:and the members of your Board for the kind indulgence which has been shown to me, particularly of late years, when I was per- mitted to continue as a Commissioner attending at meet- ings when it suited my convenience. Kindly convey to my brother Commissioners, the pleasure I have in look- ing back to my connection with you and them.—Yours G. MCCORQUODALE." SCHOOL ATTENDANCE COMMITTEE.—A CORRECTION. At a meeting of this Committee the school attendance officer (Mr. Ball) read his report, and it was resolved that his correction be accepted with reference to a state- ment which appeared in his flovember report with regard to children being turned away from Haydock school. No children having been refused admission at that school by Mr. Taylor, Mr. Ball had been misin- formed and expressed his regret for the mistake. DATE OF THE NEW HOARD'S FIRST MEETING. It -vas decided to hold the first meeting of the District Council on Monday, January 7th. VOTES OF THANKS. Mr. Ratcliffe, as one of the oldest members of the Board proposed a vote of thanks to the Chairman for his conduct in the chair during the past year. He belieeed he had done his duty manfully, and he hoped that they would have as good a chairman on the new Board.—Mr. MacMahon seconded, and also spoke of Mr. Emmett's agreeable and reasonable conduct in the chair. —The motion was passed unanimously, and Mr.Emmatt in reply thanked the Board, and sale that he had tried to discharge his duties honestly and fairly without fear or favour. He hoped that the Chairman of the new Bok.rd would have as much assistance as he had had from the members. He also thought that they ought to thank their.officers, and he had therefore great pleasure in proposing a hearty vote of thanks to Mr. Worsley, Mr. Brierley, Mr. Swan, Mr. Ball, Mr. Gregory, and Mr. Houghton. He had been a member of the Board of Commissioners for 27 years, and the Board had always received much assistance from the officers.—Mr. Rat- cliffe seconded the motion.—Mr. Ormand said that he was glad that motion had been proposed by the chair- man. He had received a great deal of inspiration from the Law Clerk and Mr. Brierley, and they were always willing to give every information that was required. As they were multiplying their laws, they would no doubt require even rhore information from them in the future. He had great pleasure in supporting the resolution.—The resolution was carried unanimously.—Mr. Worsley thanked the Board very much for the resolution which they had passed. He thanked them on behalf of all the officers of the Board. They had always done their best, and would endeavour to do so in the future.—Mr. Brierley also expressed his appreciation of the vote of thanks which had been passed.—The proceedings then terminated.

NOTES BY THE WAY AT EARLESTOWN.

(BY AN OCCASIONAL CONTRIBUTOR).

The fight is o'er, the battle's lost and won, and we I are where we are, and nowhere else. The lucky bags have been emptied, and the surprise packets I predicted a few wePki back have been opened, their contents being really surprises which more than ens never anticipated. Result: Several sadder and wiser men. Sadder perhaps because of needless expense, wiser for the knowledge they gain, that promises, to use the old adage, are like pie crust. Wiser, because they learn that to run a contest of this kind single-handed is simp- ly throwing money away. An election is not won by the dissemination of litera- ture, and personal visiting of the houses of electors. An energetic agent, a good working Committee, and canvassers, are the elements of victory, and this has been exemplified in the results of the contest here

The'greatest surprise of all perhaps was in the Wargrave ward, where the three candidates put forward by the Conservative party headed the poll. Wargrave is essentially a workingmen's ward, and it is a notable fact that the committee who toiled in the interests of the two workingmen candidates, felt pretty confident of returning their candidates. The canvas showed to a certainty that they would be able to run one, and just squeeze the other in, but The,ballot decreed otherwise. It is hard to say what would have been the result had Dr. Mouncey not come forward as an Independent, but experts profess to say that it would have made no appre- ciable difference. It is a peculiar fact that, with two exceptions, the three candidates chosen for each ward, at the outset of hostilities, have been returned, the exceptions being Crow-lane, and 'Viaduct wards, one being returned OR the Catholic vote, and the other by the Conservative ticket. The next important stage will be the selection of chairman of the Council. which carries with it the honour of magistrate. If I was chosen to arbitrate I should vote solid for the chairman of the expir- ing Board, Mr. Emmett, because I think if only as a matter of courtesy, he is fully entitled to it. His position at the head of the poll, having received more votes than any other candidate in any ward, also gives him a prior claim, and, finally, no better chairman could be chosen. I hear it whispered, however, that a clique has been formed to deprive him of the honour, and if this is so, the attempt will not place the members of it in any creditable light. In the election of chairman.party questions must he put on one side, and the best man for the position elevated to it.

The question of more police was practically demonstrated on Monday, and I am sure Inspector Morgan was at his wit's end how to provide for the requirements of the Returning officer, and also look after the peace of the neighbourhood. In each booth he bad to station a policeman, and to do this he had .to draw on his outside resources, causing double duty to fall on some unfortunate officer. Eirlestewn is a growing place, and the population is becoming poportionately larger, but as far as I can gather there does not seem any inclination to increase the protection of the inhabitants. Police- men are only human, and they cannot be expected to look after us night and day. It'A a wonder we ever sleep, when we consider what golden oppor- tunities there are for burglars. We want not less than four extra policemen, and as the cost would be something like the fraction of a penny in the pound. I consider one of the first dnties of the new Council should be to apply for these extra men,and thus intimate to the inhabitants that they are care- ful for their safety.

===== 0002601_18941222_art0144.txt | included=true | included =====
THE CHRISTMNS-PA.NTOMIME AT ST. HELENS.

"DICK WHITTINGTON." The preparations for the production of the Christmas pantomime at the splendid new Theatre Royal and Opera House, St. Helens, to-morrow (Saturday) night, have mow been practically cempleted, and a brilliant success seems assured. The title of this year's entertain- ment is the familiar one of " Dick Whittington. or the Cat, the Rat and the Fairies of Bowbell," and to ensure the performance a lasting popu- larity during the festive season, Mr. Wallace Bevil!, the respected and popular proprietor, following on his custom of former years, has absolutely spared no ex- pense, while his personal efforts, supplemented by these of his energetic acting manager. Mr. Richard Soars.have been untiring. The story of " Dick Whittingten." as it will be presented to the patrons of Mr. Revill's hand 'me theatre. has been specially written by Mr. Victor Stevens, and a cursory glance at the " script shows that all the essential features of the familiar story itave been retained while additional i.nuches have been impartad to enhance its attractiveness for stage representation. The pantomime will be produced under the stage management of Mr. C. P Co,ke, a gentleman well qualified by experience for tits post, and Mr. Bevil' will personally supervise all the arrangements—a guarantee. sufficient in itself, to assure the lovers of an evening's genuine fun and entertainment, that nothing will he wanting to me( their desires. The company will include some of the best artistes in the pantomimic line now in the country, and the fact that the grand entertainment in the orien- tal palace scene will be submitted by the renowned Miss Emily Lyndale. Miss Lizzie Bonne,the brothers Passmore, Messrs. Maurice and Ross. and other eminent variety artistes indicates the almost suner-ahundance of talents at command to maintain an unfi togin2 interest in the performance. As will be noticed 'from the detailed ad- vertisement in another column, special trains and conveyances to the outlying districts have been arranged for, and there are convenient late trains to places at a greater distance. For the convenience of the younger portion of the corn rsunity, however, and to meet the wishes of persons residing in the surrounding districts, illuminated after, noon performances have been arranged, the first ot , which will be given on Wednesday next (Boxing Day) followed by others on Saturday next, Tuesday (New Year's Day), and every Saturday afternoon during the season.

Mr. John Weir was duly declared elected. Mr. 1 W. Weir acted as agent for Mr. John Weir, and Mr. S. Wright, of Warrington. for Mr. Joseph Mounfield. STRETTON. The first Parish Council meeting for Stretton was held in the schoolroom, on Monday evening. Present— the Rev. Canon Dodd, and Messrs. J. Smith. A. Faulk- ner, E lward Brocklehurst, J. Leather, James Howard, and E. Owen. The Rev. Canon Dodd was duly elected chairman. After each member present had signed a declaration of acceptance of office the following officers were elected : Vice-chairman, dr. Smith ; treasurer, Mr. Edward Brocklehurst. Mr. J. Ellison, assistant- overseer, was appointed clerk, and it was moved that his salary be fixed at -815 per annum for the whole work instead of £ll as at present. An amendment was moved that it should stand at £l3, and upon a show of hands the amendment was lost by four votes to two. ' The next mcAing of the Council was fixed to take place on the first Monday in February. A vote of thanks hav- ing been accorded to the Chairman for presiding, the meeting terminated.

WARRINGTON.

There was a great amount of interest evinced in War- rington over the election of members to the Board of Guardians. The election was conducted on much the same limes as municipal elections, there being one guardian for each ward. Out of the nine wards only three were spared a contest, these being—Tlowley, Latchford, and Be wsey wards, for which Messrs. Davies, A. Jolley, and T. Malkin were returned respectively. In the remaining wards, the elections were fought on in- dependent lines chiefly, the only candidate to he brought out by any association or party, being Mr. Arthur Lang for the St. Jelin's ward, who was a nominee of the Trades Council The result of the election has produced a marked change in the constitution of the Board. For the first time a lady will sit, and Mrs. Clay has also established a record in a ward election, by being re- turned with the enormous maj )rity of 419 votes. Mr. A. J. Jolley's maj9rity of 215 over Mr. Aukland came as a surprise to most people. A close fight was expected, but expectations were not realised, and Mr. Jolley's majority was second only to Mrs. Clay's. The Rev. W. H. Drummond made a very good fight in the Fairfield ward, and Mr. Pilling only. succeeded in keep- ing his seat by a majority of 36 votes, the smallest majority of the day. It is to be regretted that Mr. Drummond did not succeed, for he would have been an exceedingly useful member, but en the whole the results of the elections are very satisfactory.

GREAT SANKEY. The first Parish Council for Great Sankey was held in the Great Sankey Bard Schools, Mr. W. P. Skelton, by virtue of his office at the ratepayers' meeting on Dec. 4th, occupied the chair. The following gentlemen were also present—Messrs. Henry Bate, James Fairclough, George Thom pson,Thomas Halton, Thomas Kilshaw, W. H. L. Gorse, and Joseph Wright (assistant overseer). Mr. George Thompson proposed that Mr. W. P. Skelton be the chairman of the Council for the ensuing term of office.—Mr. Thomas Halton seconded the propoeition, which was cerried unanimously.—Mr. Thomas Halton was appointed honorary treasurer, on the motion of Mr. Georee Thompson, seconded by Mr. W. H. L. Gorge. Each ,JL ,t.e members of the Council was asked if they as clerk, and on receiving: no reply Mr. Joseph Wright was asked if he would act as the" hono- rary" eerk.—Mr. Wright explained that his other duties were so numerous that he would rather not do so. The question was left over until the next rate- payers' meeting, which will be held in Marsh. LATCHFORD WITHOUT.

On Tuesday night the first Parish Council meeting for Latchford Without was held in the Baptist Chapel

vestry, when the following councillors were present Messrs. George Pilling, John Lee, Henry Roberts, Geo. Nicholls, Wm. Pierpoint, and Wm. Richardson, with Mr. John Tunstall, assistant overseer. These councillors signed the usual declarations accept- ing the position to which they had been elected. To conduct the proceedings of the evening Mr. Wm. Pierpoint was elected to fill the position of chairman, en the motion of Mr. Lee, seconded by Mr. Richardson. The Chairman then said that it was now the duty of the meeting to elect a chairman who would hold office until next April, when the annual meeting would be held, and at which a permanent chairman would have te be appointed. He thought they could not do better than to choose Mr. Percy Davies in that gentleman's absence as their chairman until 1895. If they could m- duce Mr. Davies to take the position, it would, Mr. Pierpoint was sure, be a very good thing for the Connci!.—On the motion of Mr. Lee, seconded by Mr. Pilling, Mr. Percy Davies was chosen the chairman of the Coun- cil from that time till April 1895.—Mr. Roberts thought that as Mr. Davies had been appointed during his absence it was perhaps desirable that the Council should have a deputy-chairman, who could act providing Mr. Davies was not able to attend their next meeting.— Other members being of the same opinion Mr. William Pierpoint was elected deputy-chairman on the proposi- tion of Mr. Richardson, seconded by Mr. Roberts.—On the motion of Mr. Lee, seconded by Mr. Roberts, Mr. Pilling was appointed clerk to the Council without re- muneration.—Mr. Murray, of Parr's Banking Company, was chosen treasurer for the Council, on the proposition of Mr. Pilling.

TILE POLLING.

At the time of the opening of the poll on Monday, a drizzling rain was falling, and this continued during the greater part of the afternoon. This militated consider- ably against a heavy poll. Carriages were very busy, and the chief centres of activity were the Whitecross and Fairfield wards. In the other wards, the voters came up very slowly. During the afternoon, Mr. J. C. Sutton, the returning officer, visited all the polling booths in the town, and also all the country districts where there was a poll. The poll closed at eight o'clock. , and the ballot boxes were taken at once to the Town Hall, where they were counted. Mr. J. C. Sutton, Mr. J. Lyon Whittle (town clerk), Mr. Stanley Wilson (deputy town clerk), and several of the candidates were present. A little after nine o'clock, Mr. J. C. Sutton announced the result from the Town Hall steps to a large crowd of people, numbering about 1,500, which had gathered in front. The following is the result :

WARRINGTON AND -DISTRICT HOMING

SOCIETY. - ANNUAL MEETING. The above Society held its annual meeting and knife and fork tea on Monday evening at the Norton Arms Hotel, Warrington, attended by the following: H. F. Greenough, president; John Houghton, H. J. Longton, and E. Burrows, vice-presidents; 0. D. Parkinson, hon. treasurer; T. G. Grounds, eon. sec.; Messrs. Cooper, Clare Braceeirdle Timperley, Barnes, Woodward, Woodhead, Edgerley, Naylor, and Dixon. Also R. Slack, Esq. , president, Liverpool Homing Society; John Armstrong (Wigan). Fairhurst (Wigan), J. Heald (Chor- lev), J. Buckley (Huyton), R. Swann and J. Wightman (Earlestown). H. Barker (Liverpool), Wood, Moston, Addison and Wignell. After the tables had bean cleared, the Chairman, Mr. H. F. Greenough, addressed the meeting. He spoke of the good feeling and fellowship that had existed amongst ' the members during the past year, and also made refer- ence to the increase in the number of members and the proposed change in the flying route. He asked Mr. Slack of Liverpool to give his ideas of this proposed change. Mr. Slack, who spoke at great length, said that clubs which had raced from the south (Cherbourg, ' Nantes and La Rochelle) had met with continual disas- ter for years and he thought it was time some alteration took place. He advised the Warrington Club to take up the south-east route Dover, Calais, &c., along with Liverpool Clnte Mr. LONOTON also give his views of this proposed Change. He said that he was entirely opposed the view taken by Mr. Slack, and he would still support the old route. After several other members had spoken the discuseion was allowed to drop until the next meet- .ing of the members. The CHAIRMAN hereupon called for the hon. secretary's report and balance sheet. Mr. T. G. GROUNDS then read the following revert : " Before reading the account of the club's receipts and expenses I should like to make a few remarks concern- ing the position, Bce. of the club at the present time, and also give some account of the year's racing. I think it hardly necessary for me to enter too much into desail, but simply give some idea of what has been done as compared with last year. "We commenced the present year with nearly double the number of members we had in 1893. a fact I think which shows the growing popularity of the sport in Warrington, hut although this increase in the member- ship the number of entries for the various races are very little better than last year. as will be seen from the fieures I shall give von presently. During the past Year the club has increased its property by adding new triet maps, several new baskets and troughs, new umnire box, fee. On looking over these I find the rock worth enmethine litre from £1.5 to £2O. This is a con- siderable item of 'satins, and it behoves every member to See that the greatest care is taken of the same. "For the reef two years the M.F.C.have been engaged to liberate the club's hircis in the various races at a fee of £5 for England. This I consider a very rensinable figure when the work is done satisfactorily, but when it is done as it has been this year! censidrr it. the reverse. The member. are well aware of the inconvenience and loss of time they have been put to through our being wrongly advised of the time of liberation in several of the races. In the Swindon race for old birds you will remember the birds were not liberated until the second day through an omission on the part of the M.F.C. con- voyer, although we were advised that the birds were liberated on the first day. In the youneer birds race from Worcester you will remember the birds were liberated and arrived home early in the day without our being advised of the time of liberation. We all know how the Ventnor race ended. The season was com- menced with the usual training stage for 0.8. from Stafford, but as; this turned out a failure the committee deemed it advicable to abandon the same training stage for Y.B. The Out) has held five 0.8. races, Worcester, Swindon, Ventnor. Valognes, and Avranehes. The number of entries for these races have been 875 from 20 members, as compared with 768 entries from 13 mem- bers in 1893. The winners of the various 0.13. races are as follows :—Worceeter : 1, H. Clare ; 2, H. Bracegird'e; 3. R. Barnes. Swindon : 1, R. Barnes; 2, H. J. Lone- ton ;3, E. Burrows. Ventnor :1, E. Burrows ;2, H. Clare; 3, H. Clare. Valoenes . 1, E. Burrows; 2, A. Buckley; 3, J Horton. Avranches : 1, A. Buckley; 2, H F. G ; 3, E. Burrows. Averaee velocities of OR. races: 1,, E. Burrows, 856 yards ; 2, A. Buckley, 697 yards ; 3, H. Clare. 695 yards. We had three races for 1Y.8., Worcester. Swindon. and Salisbury. The number of entries was 505 from 18 members, as compared with 704 entries from 12 members in 1893. The winners of Ithe Y.B. races have been as follows :—Worcester : 1 and 2, H. S. Longton ' • 3, E. Burrows. Swindon: 1. H. Clare ;2, C. D. Parkinson ;3, H. F. Greenough. Salis- bury : 1, H. J. Loneton ; 2, J. Cooper ; 3,H. J. Longten. Average velocities in Y.B. races :-I,H. J.Longton 1,058; 2, H. F. Greenouth, 1,0031 ;3, C. D. Parkinson, 1,008. A greater amount of interest has been shown by the younger fanciers this year than last as special prizea have been given to novices for the best averages in the English races for 0. and Y. birds. The prizes, gold medal presented by the President, gold centre medal presented by Mr. E. Burrows, silver medal presented by myself have been won as follows :-1, F. C. Dixen. veto. 887; 2, ft. Barnes, veln. 872; 3. J. James, velo. 863. Mr. A. Buckley wins Mr. Moore's special, a pair of equen- kers. for winning the kvranches race. Mr. E. COUNTY OF CHESTER, TO wir. NOTICE IS HEREBY GIVEN, that the next GENERAL QUARTER SESSIONS of the Peace for the COUNTY OF CHESTER will be holden at the CASTLE OF CHZSTER, in and for the said County, on MONDAY, tho 31st day of December instant, at Half-past Eleven o'clock in the Forenoon, when the Grand Jury will be empannelled. The hearing of all Appeals arising within the Hundreds of Broxton, Eddisbury, Nantwich' and Wirral, forming the Chester District, and the trials of all prisoners com- mitted from such Hundreds, will take place at such Sessions. The Grand and Petty Jury for the Chester District must be in attendance at the Castle of Cbester, at the opening of the Court on MONDAY, the 31st day of December instant, at Half-past Eleven o'clock in the Forenoon. THE Court will be Adjourned to the Court House, Nether Knutsford, on WEDNESDAY, the 2nd day of January next, at Eleven o'clock in the Forenoon. The hearing of all Appeals arising within the Hnndreds of Bucklow, Macclesfield, and Northwich, forming the Knutsford District, and the trials of all prisoners com- mitted from the last-mentioned Hundreds will take place at such Adjourned Sessions. The Grand and Petty Jury for the Knutsford District must be in attendance at the Court House, Knutsford, at the opeiing of the Court on WEDNESDAY, the 2nd day of January next, at Eleven o'clock in the Forenoon. REGIN ALD POTTS, Clerk of the Peace for the County of Chester. Chester, 11th December, 1894.

TO THE ELECTORS OF WESTON. THE VICARAGE. RUNCORN. THE VICAR wishes to thank those (-lectors of Weston who so kindly gave him their votes. It is a pleasure to him be think that he received support from Wesleyana and Roman Catholics (as he is assured was the case) as well as from mem- bers of the Church of England. The good feeling shown was most gratifying. and it is to be hoped that this beginning is a good augury for the work of the Parish Council in the future. -

The extensive alterations necessitated by continued increase of business at the Manchester Louvre having now been almost cetnpleted, the Proprietors have pleasure in informing their old-established" clientele" and the public generally, that the increased Exhibition Space In their Show-rooms (opposite All Saints' Church) has enabled them to stock and display an enormously added Quantity of FURNITURE—both New and Second-hand—BO that their Cnstorners have now a Collection to choose from which itts not too much to say is second to none in the United Kingdom. The recent extensive Sales held by us in consequence of Extension of Premises met with such a ready response from wide-awake clients, that our former Stock was speedily reduced and it became a serious question with us how to cope with the demands of fresh Customers. TO THE ELECTORS AND FRIENDS OF WHITBCROSS WARD. LIDINS AND GENTLEMEN,— It is very gratifying that on the first occasion when the suffrages of the electors have been asked by a woman ,they have responded so decisively, and have shown how kindly they appreciate the desirability of the claims and interests of women being represented on the Board. To the Is ies belonging to both political parties I cannot find words to adequately express my deep sense of gratitude for their labours both on the day of the election and for some days previously, and to the men who worked as members of my committee, and who in this matter set aside their own especial claims in order to help the women of Warrington, I am deeply thankful. I feel now that it has become a sacred duty to me, that no effort shall be wanting on my part to instil), the confidence which has been so generously bestowed with regard to my- self, and I shall on all occasions do everything possible to carry out the pledges which I have already given. I remain, Yours truly, LEAH CLAY.

Avoid salt and all condi- istents; flesh meat, pastry, tea and coffee, and all hot viands, the latter being most damaging to the complex- ion. The diet should consist largely of ripe fruit (oranges do very well), also whole-wheat-meal bread and oatmeal. Honey, sugar, and milk should be used in moderation. The prices charged for the goods are en, rilious, without any regard for quality, the agreements are one-sided, being drawn with a cuteness which would do credit to a money lender, full of despotic and arbitrary restrictions, suchas not to remove the mark on the furniture, ni t to remove the furniture, not to wear the furniture. At all times to allow the mere,- naries to inspect the furniture. To pay the instal- ments regularly, and if onels missed to forfeit the furniture and all the moneys you may have paid, that is to say, although you might have paid £l9 on £2 worth of furniture, but mksed the last payinent by a single day, the vultures could emp'y sour house and stick to the £l9 izeid, as well as the furniture.
